Beyond Softmax and Entropy: Improving Convergence Guarantees of Policy Gradients by 𝒇 \boldsymbol{f} -SoftArgmax Parameterization with Coupled Regularization
Safwan Labbi, Daniil Tiapkin, Paul Mangold, Eric Moulines

Abstract
Policy gradient methods are known to be highly sensitive to the choice of policy parameterization. In particular, the widely used softmax parameterization can induce ill-conditioned optimization landscapes and lead to exponentially slow convergence. Although this can be mitigated by preconditioning, this solution is often computationally expensive. Instead, we propose replacing the softmax with an alternative family of policy parameterizations based on the generalized f f - softargmax . We further advocate coupling this parameterization with a regularizer induced by the same f f -divergence, which improves the optimization landscape and ensures that the resulting regularized objective satisfies a Polyak–Łojasiewicz inequality. Leveraging this structure, we establish the first explicit non-asymptotic last-iterate convergence guarantees for stochastic policy gradient methods for finite MDPs without any form of preconditioning . We also derive sample-complexity bounds for the unregularized problem and show that f f -PG with Tsallis divergences achieves polynomial sample complexity in contrast to the exponential complexity incurred by the standard softmax parameterization.

to policy gradient methods that do not use any form of preconditionning;
{(2)}

Table

);

(4)

(5)

indicates an explicit convergence rate with exponential dependence on at least one parameter.

Policy gradient methods are a cornerstone of modern reinforcement learning (RL) and underpin many of its most notable successes. Algorithms such as Trust-Region Policy Optimization (TRPO;
31
) and Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO;
32
) have demonstrated strong empirical performance across a wide range of domains
[
3
,
2
]
. Despite these successes, it has become increasingly clear that the performance and convergence behavior of policy gradient methods are highly sensitive to seemingly low-level design choices, among which the choice of policy parameterization plays a central role
[
11
]
.

In discrete control scenarios, the default choice is the softmax parameterization, typically coupled with entropy regularization.
While ubiquitous, several recent results have revealed fundamental limitations of softmax-based policy gradient methods
[
20
,
15
]
. In particular, in the absence of regularization, the softmax parameterization can induce extremely flat regions in the optimization landscape, leading to an unavoidable exponential lower bound on the rate of convergence
[
15
]
. Although entropy regularization is sometimes introduced in an attempt to mitigate this issue, no polynomial convergence guarantees are currently known in this setting. Even with entropy regularization, the landscape remains flat (see
Figure

1(a)
for an illustration). These observations motivate treating the policy parameterization itself as a
design choice
: rather than varying the regularizer within softmax, we ask whether moving beyond softmax can fundamentally improve the conditioning of policy gradient methods.

In this paper, we follow the line of work of
[
20
,
17
]
and propose a new flexible family of alternative parameterizations induced by divergence generators (denoted
f
in the following), which we refer to as
f
-softargmax parameterizations
. We regularize the objective with the corresponding
f
-divergence, and we refer to this as a
coupled
parameterization–regularization pair (i.e., the same generator
f
induces both the parameterization and the regularizer). This viewpoint generalizes the classical softmax–entropy pairing, in which the policy is both induced and regularized by Shannon entropy.
Similar constructions have recently shown theoretical and practical benefits for supervised learning
[
4
,
30
]
, but remain largely unexplored in reinforcement learning.
This takes policy gradient methods
beyond the softmax and its coupled entropy regularization
, yielding a better conditioned optimization landscape (see
Figure

1(b)
).
In particular, we show that when these parameterizations are
coupled
with the regularizer induced by the corresponding
f
-divergence, policy gradient methods enjoy improved convergence rates.
Remarkably, for the Tsallis divergence, this leads to convergence rates exponentially faster compared to the softmax–entropy pairing.

Formally, we study the
f
-regularized value function under the
f
-softargmax parameterization. We show that it satisfies a
non-uniform Łojasiewicz inequality
and a monotonicity property.
This monotonicity allows us to restrict the optimization to regions that are easy to project onto and in which the Łojasiewicz coefficient is uniformly lower bounded, resulting in the
uniform Polyak-Łojasiewicz
inequality over the region of interest. Building on these observations, we establish
global last-iterate convergence guarantees
for stochastic policy gradient methods in the tabular setting, with
fully explicit constants
. To the best of our knowledge, these are the first guarantees of this type for policy gradient methods, even with entropy regularization and softmax parameterization, that do not rely on preconditioning or exponentially large batch sizes.
For the KL-induced parameterization–regularization pair (softmax–entropy), the resulting uniform Polyak–Łojasiewicz constant is exponentially small in the problem parameters, recovering known exponential convergence rates
[
8
]
. In contrast, for Tsallis divergence generators, this constant scales only polynomially, reflecting a substantially better-conditioned optimization landscape.
Additionally, our analysis shows that moving beyond the entropy-softmax pairing yields a better trade-off between regularization bias and sample complexity. In particular,
Tsallis-type couplings yield
polynomial
last-iterate convergence guarantees even for the
unregularized
objective, improving upon the worst-case guarantees known for the standard softmax (see
Table

1
).

Overall, our contributions are threefold:

•

We introduce
f
-softargmax policy parameterizations and study the regularity of the associated
f
-regularized value function as a function of the policy parameters. We show that it is smooth, satisfies a non-uniform Łojasiewicz inequality, and, by exploiting a monotonicity property, admits a uniform bound on a Polyak-Łojasiewicz constant on a region of interest that is easy to project onto.

•

We prove global last-iterate convergence guarantees for stochastic policy gradient in the tabular setting, with fully explicit sample complexity bounds that apply to both regularized and unregularized objectives.

•

We demonstrate that alternative couplings beyond entropy–softmax lead to improved sample complexity for an unregularized problem both theoretically and empirically. In particular, the Tsallis coupling yields polynomial dependencies on problem parameters, resulting in an exponential improvement over softmax, and provides additional flexibility for practical adaptation.

.

The paper is organized as follows.
Section

2
introduces the necessary background.
Section

3
presents the
f
-softargmax parameterization and the properties of the
f
-regularized value under this parameterization. Convergence rates for policy gradient are established in
Section

4
, and numerical experiments are reported in
Section

5
.

Related Work.

(Policy gradient methods.)

Global convergence guarantees are known to hold for unregularized policy gradient methods with deterministic gradients, achieving sublinear rates with constant step-sizes
[
21
,
18
]
. However, the convergence rates of softmax-based policy gradient methods is exponential in the problem parameters
[
20
,
15
]
. Two strategies were proposed to mitigate this issue: first, preconditioning, most notably through natural policy gradient methods
[
13
]
, which can alleviate ill-conditioning, but scales poorly to larger problems due to the nature of the updates. Second, log-barrier regularization
[
36
]
, which yields polynomial rates but has no last iterate convergence guarantees and is unstable in practice. In contrast, our approach avoids preconditioning altogether and therefore retains the scalability of standard policy gradient methods, while providing explicit polynomial convergence guarantees for the
last iterate
.

(Alternative Parameterizations.)

Alternatives to softmax have been proposed and studied in optimization
[
19
,
27
,
30
]
. In RL, the study of alternative parameterizations is still in its early stages. The escort transform of
20
avoids exponential slowdowns in deterministic settings, but its guarantees rely on increasing step-sizes and do not extend to stochastic gradients. The Hadamard parameterization
[
17
]
yields local linear convergence in deterministic regimes, but without explicit constants.
In this work, we propose a more flexible family of parameterizations that can adapt to various problems and provide explicit guarantees in the stochastic setting.

2
Background

Reinforcement Learning.

Consider a discounted Markov decision process

\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\gamma,\mathsf{P},\mathsf{r},\rho)

with finite state and action spaces
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{A}
, discount factor

\gamma\in(0,1)
, transition kernel
\mathsf{P}\colon\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
,
bounded reward function
\mathsf{r}\colon\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\to[0,1]
,
and initial distribution
\rho
. The value of a policy
\pi\colon\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})
is defined by

\displaystyle\textstyle v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi}(s):=\mathbb{E}_{s}^{\pi}\left[\sum\nolimits_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\mathsf{r}(S_{t},A_{t})\right],

(1)

where
S_{0}=s
,
A_{t}\sim\pi(\cdot|S_{t})
, and
S_{t+1}\sim\mathsf{P}(\cdot|S_{t},A_{t})
. For
\rho\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
, we define
v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi}(\rho):=\sum_{s}\rho(s)v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi}(s)
. For any
\pi
, we define a corresponding discounted occupancy measure

\textstyle d_{\rho}^{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi}(s):=\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\mathbb{E}_{s}^{\pi}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\mathsf{1}_{s}(S_{t})\right]\kern 5.0pt.

Parameterizations on the simplex.
Following

30
, we study a family of parameterizations of the simplex based on divergence generators. For a given
generator

f\colon(0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
strictly convex with
f(1)=0
, and reference distribution
q
with
full support
, we define

\displaystyle\textstyle\operatorname{f-softmax}(x,q)

\displaystyle\textstyle:=\max\limits_{\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})}\big\{\langle\nu,x\rangle-\operatorname{D}^{f}(\nu\|q)\big\}\kern 5.0pt,

\displaystyle\textstyle\operatorname{f-softargmax}(x,q)

\displaystyle\textstyle:=\argmax\limits_{\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})}\big\{\langle\nu,x\rangle-\operatorname{D}^{f}(\nu\|q)\big\}\kern 5.0pt,

where
\operatorname{D}^{f}(p\|q)
is the
f
-divergence between
p
and
q
(see
6
, or
Appendix

A
).
Since
\operatorname{D}^{f}(p\|q)
is strictly convex in its first argument on the simplex, the output of the
\operatorname{f-softargmax}
operator is well defined and unique, as it corresponds to the
\argmax
of a strictly concave function over a compact set. This construction recovers the classical softmax as a special case and yields a rich family of alternative parameterizations (see
Table

2
). Computing
\operatorname{f-softargmax}
reduces to solving a one-dimensional root-finding problem, which can be done efficiently by dichotomy; see
30
and
Lemma

B.1
for details.

\boldsymbol{f}
-Regularized Value Functions.
Given a reference policy
\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}
, temperature

\lambda\geq 0
, and a divergence generator
f
, the
f
-regularized value function of

\pi
is defined by

\displaystyle\textstyle\!v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi}^{f}(s)\!:=\!\mathbb{E}_{s}^{\pi}\!\!\left[\sum\limits_{t=0}^{\infty}\!\gamma^{t}(\mathsf{r}(S_{t},A_{t})\!-\!\lambda\operatorname{D}^{f}(\pi(\cdot|S_{t})\|\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(\cdot|S_{t}))\right],

A key result
[
9
]
is that the optimal regularized value
\smash{v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}^{f}(s):=\max_{\pi}v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi}^{f}(s)}
, together with optimal policy
\smash{\pi_{\star}^{f}}
admits a closed-form Bellman characterization:

\displaystyle\textstyle v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}^{f}(s)=\max\limits_{\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})}\{\langle\nu,q_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}^{f}(s,\cdot)\rangle-\lambda\,\operatorname{D}^{f}(\nu\|\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(\cdot|s))\},

(2)

\displaystyle\textstyle\hskip-11.00008pt\pi_{\star}^{f}(\cdot|s)=\!\argmax\limits_{\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})}\{\langle\nu,q_{\hskip 0.5581pt\star}^{f}(s,\cdot)\rangle\!-\!\lambda\,\operatorname{D}^{f}(\nu\|\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(\cdot|s))\},

(3)

where
q_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}^{f}(s,a):=\mathsf{r}(s,a)+\gamma\mathsf{P}v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}^{f}(s,a)
.

3
​Coupling Parameterization ​& ​Regularization

We introduce a new class of policy parameterizations for reinforcement learning, which we refer to as
\operatorname{f-softargmax}
policies. Let
\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}
denote a full-support reference policy. For
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{S}||\mathcal{A}|}
, we define the
\operatorname{f-softargmax}
policy by

\displaystyle\!\!\pi_{\theta}^{f}(\cdot|s):=\operatorname{f-softargmax}(\theta(s,\cdot),\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(\cdot|s)),\kern 5.0pt\forall s\in\mathcal{S}.

(4)

This parameterization can be directly used within unregularized policy gradient methods (see
Appendix

G
for further discussion). However, in practice, unregularized methods tend to over-exploit and converge prematurely to suboptimal policies. This suggests that the choice of parameterization should be guided by the geometry of a suitably regularized objective, rather than considered in isolation.

To understand which regularization is naturally associated with the
\operatorname{f-softargmax}
family, we examine the structure of the
f
-regularized problem (
3
). The optimal policy of this problem admits the following representation:

\displaystyle\pi_{\star}^{f}(\cdot|s)=\operatorname{f-softargmax}\!\big(q_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}^{f}(s,\cdot)/\lambda,\;\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(\cdot|s)\big)\kern 5.0pt.

In particular, if we choose logits
\theta_{\star}^{f}(s,\cdot)=q_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}^{f}(s,\cdot)/\lambda+b(s)
, where
b\colon\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}
is an arbitrary state-dependent baseline, then the
\operatorname{f-softargmax}
mapping exactly recovers the optimal
f
-regularized policy, i.e.,
\pi_{\theta_{\star}}^{f}=\pi_{\star}^{f}
.

This shows that the
\operatorname{f-softargmax}
parameterization is not arbitrary: it is precisely matched to the geometry of the
f
-regularized problem. Under this parameterization, learning the policy is equivalent to learning the regularized optimal
Q
-function, and the associated
f
-divergence regularizer arises naturally from the variational characterization of the optimal policy.

To further formalize the benefits of such coupling, we now establish the smoothness, as well as a Polyak-Łojasiewicz inequality, of the
f
-regularized value with coupled parameterization
v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}^{f}:=v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi_{\theta}^{f}}^{f}
.
We derive these properties under the following two assumptions on
f
and
\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}
.

Assumption
\textbf{P}(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})
.

There exists a number
\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}>0
such that
\min_{(s,a)}\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(a|s)>\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}
.

Assumption
\textbf{A}_{f}(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})
.

The generator function
f
satisfies:

(i)

f
is bounded and strictly convex on
[0;1/\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}]
,
f(1)=0
, and is thrice differentiable on
(0,1/\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})
;

(ii)

\lim_{u\downarrow 0^{+}}f^{\prime}(u)\!=\!-\infty
, and
\lim_{u\rightarrow 0}|f^{\prime}(u)/f^{\prime\prime}(u)|\!<\!\infty
;

(iii)

there exists
\omega_{f}\in[1,\infty)
, and
\kappa_{f}\in(0,\infty)
, such that for any
u\in[0;1/\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}]
, we have
\smash{1/(uf^{\prime\prime}(u))\leq\omega_{f},\text{ and }|f^{\prime\prime\prime}(u)/f^{\prime\prime}(u)^{2}|\leq\kappa_{f}}
;

(iv)

there exists
\iota_{f}\in(0,1]
such that
f^{\prime\prime}
decreases on
[0;\iota_{f}]
and for any
u\in[\iota_{f};1/\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}]
,
f^{\prime\prime}(\iota_{f})\geq f^{\prime\prime}(u)
.

These conditions are met by a broad class of commonly used divergence generators, like the KL, Tsallis with
\alpha\leq 1
, and Jensen-Shannon (see
Appendix

F
).

Remark 3.1

Tsallis divergences with
\alpha>1
violate condition (ii): since
f^{\prime}(0)
is finite, the induced policies are sparse, leading to non-smooth parameterizations. We leave the extension to this setting for future work.

Under
\textbf{P}(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})
and
\textbf{A}_{f}(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})
, we can define the weights
\operatorname{w}_{\theta}^{f}(a|s)
and the sum
\operatorname{W}_{\theta}^{f}(s)
, defined as

\displaystyle\textstyle\operatorname{w}_{\theta}^{f}(a|s):=\textstyle\frac{1}{\operatorname{W}_{\theta}^{f}(s)}\frac{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(a|s)}{f^{\prime\prime}(\pi_{\theta}^{f}(a|s)/\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(a|s))}\kern 5.0pt,

(5)

\displaystyle\textstyle\quad\text{ with }\operatorname{W}_{\theta}^{f}(s):=\textstyle\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\frac{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(a|s)}{f^{\prime\prime}(\pi_{\theta}^{f}(a|s)/\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(a|s))}\kern 5.0pt,

(6)

which will play a central role in our analysis.
In the KL divergence case, we recover simple expressions
\smash{\operatorname{w}_{\theta}^{f}(a|s)\equiv\pi_{\theta}^{f}(a|s)}
and
\smash{\operatorname{W}_{\theta}^{f}(s)=1}
.
Using the notations in (
5
), we can express the gradient of the regularized value.

Lemma 3.2
.

Assume that, for some
\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}>0
,
f
and
\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}
satisfy
\textbf{A}_{f}(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})
and
\textbf{P}(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})
. For any
s\in\mathcal{S}
, we have

\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{\partial v_{\hskip 0.51534pt\theta}^{f}(\rho)}{\partial\theta(s,\cdot)}\!=\!\operatorname{W}_{\theta}^{f}(s)\frac{d_{\rho}^{\hskip 0.51534pt\theta}(s)}{1-\gamma}H(\operatorname{w}_{\theta}^{f}(\cdot|s))\left[q_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}^{f}(s,\cdot)-\lambda\theta(s,\cdot)\right],

where for any vector
u\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{A}|}
,
H(u):=\diag(u)-uu^{\top}
,
q_{\hskip 0.57367pt}^{f}:=\mathsf{r}+\gamma\mathsf{P}v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}^{f}
, and
d_{\rho}^{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}(s):=d_{\rho}^{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi_{\theta}^{f}}(s)
.

Next, we introduce three quantities that that arise naturally in the expression of the Hessian of

v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}^{f}(\rho)
.

\displaystyle\mathrm{y}_{f}

\displaystyle:=\textstyle\max\limits_{(s,\nu)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})}\!\sum\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\!\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(a|s)\frac{\left|f^{\prime}\left(\nu(a)/\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(a|s)\right)\right|}{f^{\prime\prime}\left(\nu(a)/\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(a|s)\right)},

(7)

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathrm{d}_{f}

\displaystyle:=\textstyle\max\limits_{(s,\nu)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})}\operatorname{D}^{f}(\nu\|\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(\cdot|s))\kern 5.0pt,

(8)

\displaystyle\zeta_{f}

\displaystyle:=\textstyle\min\limits_{(s,\nu)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})}\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\frac{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(a|s)}{f^{\prime\prime}(\nu(a)/\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(a|s))}>0\kern 5.0pt.

(9)

As the previous quantities are bounded under
\textbf{A}_{f}(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})
and
\textbf{P}(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})
, we can establish the smoothness of
\smash{v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}^{f}(\rho)}
.

Theorem 3.3
.

Assume
\textbf{A}_{f}(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})
and
\textbf{P}(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})
for some
\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}>0
. For any
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{S}||\mathcal{A}|}
,
\left\|\smash{\nabla^{2}v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}^{f}(\rho)}\right\|_{2}\leq\smash{L_{f}}
with

\displaystyle\textstyle L_{f}

\displaystyle\textstyle:=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\omega_{f}^{2}+\omega_{f}\kappa_{f}+\lambda\cdot(\omega_{f}^{2}\mathrm{d}_{f}+\omega_{f}(\kappa_{f}\mathrm{d}_{f}+\mathrm{y}_{f})+\omega_{f}+2\kappa_{f}\mathrm{y}_{f})}{(1-\gamma)^{3}}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

We refer to
Section

B.4
for a proof and a complete expression of
L_{f}
.
We now introduce the classical exploration assumption
[
20
,
21
,
1
]
.

Assumption
\textbf{A}_{\rho}
.

​The coefficient
\rho_{\min}:=\min_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\rho(s)
of the initial distribution
\rho
satisfies
\rho_{\min}\!>0
.

Next, we derive a Non-Uniform Łojasiewicz inequality.

Theorem 3.4
.

Assume that, for some
\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}>0
,
\textbf{A}_{f}(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})
and
\textbf{P}(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})
hold. Assume in addition that the initial distribution
\rho
satisfies
\textbf{A}_{\rho}
. Then, it holds that

\displaystyle\left\|\smash{\nabla_{\theta}v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}^{f}(\rho)}\right\|_{2}^{2}\geq\mu_{f}(\theta)(v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}^{f}(\rho)-v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}^{f}(\rho))\kern 5.0pt,

with
\mu_{f}(\theta):=\lambda(1-\gamma)\rho_{\min}^{2}(\zeta_{f}/\omega_{f})^{2}\min_{s,a}\operatorname{w}_{\theta}^{f}(a|s)^{2}
.

We prove this theorem in
Appendix

C
.
To highlight the main steps of the proof, we give a sketch of the proof in the bandits setting, where the state space is a singleton.

Sketch of the proof in the bandits case.

The proof consists of two steps:
(1)
we bound the sub-optimality gap by the distance between the logit and the rescaled reward;
(2)
then link it to the gradient of the function.

Step 1:

By (
2
), the optimal regularized value is equal to

\textstyle v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}^{f}=\lambda\operatorname{f-softmax}(\mathsf{r}/\lambda,\pi_{\operatorname{ref}})
.
Next, since
\operatorname{f-softmax}(\theta,\pi_{\operatorname{ref}})=\langle\pi_{\theta}^{f},\theta\rangle-\operatorname{D}^{f}(\pi_{\theta}^{f}\|\pi_{\operatorname{ref}})
, and
v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}^{f}\;=\;\langle\pi_{\theta}^{f},\mathsf{r}\rangle-\lambda\operatorname{D}^{f}(\pi_{\theta}^{f}\|\pi_{\operatorname{ref}})
, the suboptimality gap rewrite as

\displaystyle\textstyle v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}^{f}-v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}^{f}=\lambda\operatorname{f-softmax}(\frac{\mathsf{r}}{\lambda},\pi_{\operatorname{ref}})\!-\!\langle\pi_{\theta}^{f},\mathsf{r}\rangle\!+\!\lambda\operatorname{D}^{f}(\pi_{\theta}^{f}\|\pi_{\operatorname{ref}})

\displaystyle\!\!\textstyle=\!\lambda\big[\operatorname{f-softmax}(\frac{\mathsf{r}}{\lambda},\!\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}\!)\!-\!\operatorname{f-softmax}(\theta,\pi_{\operatorname{ref}})\!-\!\langle\pi_{\theta}^{f},\frac{\mathsf{r}}{\lambda}\!-\!\theta\rangle\!\big].

Combining
\pi_{\theta}^{f}\!=\!\nabla\operatorname{f-softmax}(\theta,\pi_{\operatorname{ref}})
, and that for
a\in\mathbb{R}
,
\operatorname{f-softmax}(\theta+\alpha\mathsf{1}_{|\mathcal{A}|},\pi_{\operatorname{ref}})=\operatorname{f-softmax}(\theta,\pi_{\operatorname{ref}})+\alpha
yields

\displaystyle\textstyle\!v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}^{f}\!\!-\!v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}^{f}\!\!=\!\!\lambda\big[\!\operatorname{f-softmax}(\frac{\mathsf{r}}{\lambda},\!\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}\!)\!-\!\operatorname{f-softmax}(\theta\!+\!K_{\theta}^{f}\mathsf{1}_{|\mathcal{A}|},\!\pi_{\operatorname{ref}})

\displaystyle\textstyle-\langle\nabla\operatorname{f-softmax}(\theta+K_{\theta}^{f}\mathsf{1}_{|\mathcal{A}|},\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}),\mathsf{r}/\lambda-\theta-K_{\theta}^{f}\mathsf{1}_{|\mathcal{A}|}\rangle\big],

where we have defined
K_{\theta}^{f}=\langle\mathsf{r}/\lambda-\theta,\mathsf{1}_{|\mathcal{A}|}\rangle/|\mathcal{A}|
. Defining
\zeta_{\theta}^{f}=\mathsf{r}/\lambda-\theta-K_{\theta}^{f}\mathsf{1}_{|\mathcal{A}|}
and using a second-order Taylor expansion of the function

\operatorname{f-softmax}(\cdot)
between, we obtain

\displaystyle\textstyle v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}^{f}-v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}^{f}=\tfrac{\lambda}{2}(\zeta_{\theta}^{f})^{\top}\nabla^{2}\operatorname{f-softmax}_{f}(\xi)\zeta_{\theta}^{f},

(10)

for some
\xi
on the segment joining
\theta+K_{\theta}^{f}\mathsf{1}_{|\mathcal{A}|}
and
r/\lambda
. Next, by
Lemma

B.5
, it holds that
\left\|\nabla^{2}\operatorname{f-softmax}(\xi)\right\|_{2}\leq 2\omega_{f}
, which implies
v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}^{f}-v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}^{f}\;\leq\;\lambda\omega_{f}\|\zeta_{\theta}^{f}\|_{2}^{2}
.

Step 2:
Using
Lemma

3.2
, we have
\nabla v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}^{f}=\operatorname{W}_{\theta}^{f}H(\operatorname{w}_{\theta}^{f})\left[\mathsf{r}-\lambda\theta\right]
. Next, applying
Lemma 23 of
21
(see
Lemma

I.4
) gives

\|\nabla v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}^{f}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\operatorname{W}_{\theta}^{f}\min_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\operatorname{w}_{\theta}^{f}\|\zeta_{\theta}^{f}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\zeta_{f}\min_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\operatorname{w}_{\theta}^{f}\|\zeta_{\theta}^{f}\|_{2}^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

where by
Lemma

B.6
, we have
\smash{\operatorname{W}_{\theta}^{f}\geq\zeta_{f}}
. Finally, combining the two previous bounds proves the result

\square

For softmax parameterization coupled with entropy regularization, we retrieve a property outlined by
[
21
]
. However, their proof is highly specific to the entropy-softmax pairing case and cannot be extended to general

f
-divergences, because it relies in an essential way on the logarithm’s special properties. Indeed, their proof require rewriting the soft sub-optimality gap

v_{\star}^{\text{\tiny{KL}}}(\rho)-v_{\theta}^{\text{\tiny{KL}}}(\rho)
as
\frac{\lambda}{1-\gamma}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}d_{\rho}^{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}(s^{\prime})\operatorname{D}^{\text{\tiny{KL}}}(\pi^{\text{\tiny{KL}}}_{\theta}(\cdot|s^{\prime})\|\pi_{\star}^{\text{\tiny{KL}}}(\cdot|s))
which is not possible for a general
f
.
Our proof is more natural, as it simply relies on Taylor expansion to obtain the inequality (
10
), rather than specific properties of the KL divergence.

From Non-Uniform Łojasiewicz to Polyak-Łojasiewicz.

To obtain a Polyak-Łojasiewicz bound, we need to bound the coefficient
\mu_{f}(\theta)
uniformly.
To this end, we restrict the optimization to a smaller subspace, eliminating policies for which the regularization is too large.
Given a policy
\pi
and
\smash{0<\tau<\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}/2}
, we define the following projection-like operator
\mathcal{U}_{\tau}
, which for every
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
gives

\textstyle\!\mathcal{U}_{\tau}(\pi)(a|s)\!\!=\!\!\begin{cases}\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(a|s)\tau,&\text{if }\pi(a|s)\!\leq\!\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(a|s)\tau/2,\\
\pi(a|s)-\textstyle b_{\pi}(s),&\text{if }a=a_{\pi}^{\max}(s),\\
\pi(a|s),&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

where we define
\smash{b_{\pi}(s)\!\!=\!\ \sum_{b\in\mathcal{A}_{\tau}^{\pi}(s)}\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(b|s)\tau-\pi(b|s)}
,
\smash{a_{\pi}^{\max}(s)=\argmax_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\{\pi(a|s)/\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(a|s)\}}
, where ties in the
\argmax
are resolved arbitrary, and

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathcal{A}_{\tau}^{\pi}(s):=\left\{a\in\mathcal{A},\pi(a|s)/\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(a|s)\leq\tau/2\right\}\kern 5.0pt.

This operator prevents policies from becoming ”too deterministic”: if the probability of any action gets too close to zero, it is increased above a threshold that depends on
\tau
and
\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}
. For a proper choice of
\tau
, applying this operator on a policy returns a policy with a higher regularized value.

Theorem 3.5
.

Assume that, for some
\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}>0
,
f
and
\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}
satisfy
\textbf{A}_{f}(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})
and
\textbf{P}(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})
, and that
\rho
satisfies
\textbf{A}_{\rho}
.
Let

\displaystyle\!\tau_{\lambda}\!=\!\min([f^{\prime}]^{-1}(-\tfrac{16+8\gamma\lambda\mathrm{d}_{f}}{\lambda(1-\gamma)^{2}\rho_{\min}}),[f^{\prime}]^{-1}(-4|f^{\prime}(\tfrac{1}{2})|),\tfrac{\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}}{2})\kern 5.0pt.

Then, for any policy
\pi
and for
\widetilde{\pi}=\mathcal{U}_{\tau_{\lambda}}(\pi)
, it holds that
v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\widetilde{\pi}}^{f}(\rho)\geq v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi}^{f}(\rho)
and that
\widetilde{\pi}(a|s)\geq\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}\tau_{\lambda}
.

Since
\mathcal{U}_{\tau}
operates in the space of policies, we lift it to the parameter space by defining an operator
\mathcal{T}_{\tau}
such that for
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{S}||\mathcal{A}|}
, we have
\pi_{\mathcal{T}_{\tau}\theta}^{f}=\mathcal{U}_{\tau}\pi_{\theta}^{f}
(see
Appendix

D
for an explicit construction).
Finally, we show that with the choice of threshold from
Theorem

3.5
, we can give a uniform lower bound of
\mu_{f}
on the set of restricted logits. This shows how the non-uniform Łojasiewicz inequality is upgraded to a Polyak–Łojasiewicz condition:
it suffices to restrict the search to parameters that encode non-degenerate policies, since such policies are provably suboptimal.

Corollary 3.6
.

Assume that, for some
\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}>0
,
f
and
\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}
satisfy
\textbf{A}_{f}(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})
and
\textbf{P}(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})
and that
\rho
satisfies
\textbf{A}_{\rho}
. If

\lambda\lesssim\tfrac{1}{(1-\gamma)^{2}\rho_{\min}}\min(\tfrac{1}{|f^{\prime}(\iota_{f})|},\frac{1}{|f^{\prime}(\tfrac{1}{2})|},\frac{1}{|f^{\prime}(\tfrac{1}{2}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|})

where
\tau_{\lambda}
is defined in
Theorem

3.5
.
Under this condition, it holds that
\inf_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mu_{f}(\mathcal{T}_{\tau_{\lambda}}\theta)\geq\underline{\mu}_{f}
, where

\displaystyle\textstyle\underline{\mu}_{f}:=\lambda(1-\gamma)\rho_{\min}^{2}\zeta_{f}^{2}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{2}(f^{\star})^{\prime\prime}\big(\tfrac{-16-8\gamma\lambda\mathrm{d}_{f}}{\lambda(1-\gamma)^{2}\rho_{\min}}\big)^{2}/\omega_{f}^{2}\kern 5.0pt,

where
f^{\star}
is a convex conjugate of
f
.

4
Convergence Analysis of
\boldsymbol{f}
-PG

Algorithm 1

f
-SoftArgmax Policy Gradient

1:

Initialization:
Learning rate
\eta>0
, initial parameter
\theta_{0}
, divergence generator
f
, batch size
B
.

2:

for

t=0
to
T-1

do

3:

Collect
Z_{t}:=(S_{t,0:H-1}^{b},A_{t,0:H-1}^{b})_{b=0}^{B-1}
using
\pi_{{\theta_{t}}}^{f}

4:

Compute the gradient
\mathrm{g}_{Z_{t}}^{f}(\theta_{t})
using (
13
)

5:

Update
\theta_{t+1}=\mathcal{T}_{\tau_{\lambda}}(\theta_{t}+\eta\mathrm{g}_{Z_{t}}^{f}(\theta_{t}))

6:

Return
\theta_{T}

In this section, we aim to optimize the
f
-regularized value function under
\operatorname{f-softargmax}
parameterization.

\textstyle\max\limits_{\theta\in\Theta}\bigl\{J^{f}(\theta):=v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi_{\theta}^{f}}^{f}(\rho)\bigr\}\kern 5.0pt.

(11)

The
\boldsymbol{f}
-PG
algorithm.

We introduce
f
-PG
(
Algorithm

1
), an
f
-SoftArgmax policy gradient method with coupled regularization.
At each iteration of
f
-PG
, the agent samples a batch of independent truncated trajectories of length
H
from
\nu(\pi;\cdot)
defined for a single truncated trajectory
z=(s_{h},a_{h})_{h=0}^{H-1}\in(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A})^{H}
by
\nu(\pi;z):=\rho(s_{0})\pi(a_{0}|s_{0})\!\prod_{h=0}^{H-1}\mathsf{P}(s_{h}|s_{h-1},a_{h-1})\pi(a_{h}|s_{h})
. Then, the agent performs the update

\theta_{t+1}=\mathcal{T}_{\tau_{\lambda}}\big(\theta_{t}+\eta\cdot\mathrm{g}_{Z_{t}}^{f}(\theta_{t})\big)\kern 5.0pt,\quad\text{ for }t\geq 0\kern 5.0pt,

(12)

where
\eta>0
is a learning rate,
\mathcal{T}_{\tau_{\lambda}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{S}||\mathcal{A}|}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{S}||\mathcal{A}|}
is the projection-like operator defined in
Section

3
, and
\smash{\mathrm{g}_{Z_{t}}^{f}(\theta_{t})}
is a REINFORCE-like estimator
[
33
]
of
\smash{\nabla v_{\hskip 0.57367pt{\theta_{t}}}^{f}(\rho)}
that uses a batch of
B
independent trajectories
Z_{t}\sim[\nu(\theta_{t})]^{\otimes B}
.
For a batch of trajectories
\smash{z=(s_{0:H-1}^{b},a_{0:H-1}^{b})_{b=0}^{B-1}}
, this estimator is defined

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathrm{g}_{z}^{f}(\theta):=\frac{1}{B}\!\sum\limits_{b=0}^{B-1}\sum\limits_{h=0}^{H-1}\!\Big\{\sum\limits_{\ell=0}^{h}\!\frac{\partial\log\pi_{\theta}^{f}(a_{\ell}^{b}|s_{\ell}^{b})}{\partial\theta}\gamma^{h}\Big(\mathsf{r}(s_{h}^{b},a_{h}^{b})

\displaystyle\textstyle\quad-\lambda\operatorname{D}^{f}({\pi_{\theta}^{f}(\cdot|s_{h}^{b})}\|{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(\cdot|s_{h}^{b})})\Big)-\lambda\gamma^{h}\mathrm{F}_{\theta}^{f}(s_{h}^{b})\Big\}\,,

(13)

where
\mathrm{F}_{\theta}^{f}(s)
is a vector of size
|\mathcal{S}||\mathcal{A}|
defined by

\displaystyle\textstyle[\mathrm{F}_{\theta}^{f}(s)]_{(s^{\prime},b)}

\displaystyle:=\mathsf{1}_{s^{\prime}}(s)\operatorname{W}_{\theta}^{f}(s)\operatorname{w}_{\theta}^{f}(b|s)\delta(\pi_{\theta}^{f},s,b)\kern 5.0pt,

(14)

\displaystyle\delta(\pi_{\theta}^{f},s,b)

\displaystyle\textstyle:=\!f^{\prime}(\!\tfrac{\pi_{\theta}^{f}(b|s)}{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(b|s)}\!)\!-\!\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\!\operatorname{w}_{\theta}^{f}(a|s)f^{\prime}\!(\frac{\pi_{\theta}^{f}(a|s)}{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(a|s)}).

Remark 4.1

(Connection with (Lazy) Mirror Descent.)

We stress that
f
-PG
is fundamentally different from mirror descent.
With
\Phi(\pi)=\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\operatorname{D}^{f}(\pi(\cdot|s)\|\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(\cdot|s))
, the iterations of mirror descent are

\displaystyle\textstyle\nabla\Phi(\widetilde{\pi}_{t+1})

\displaystyle=\nabla\Phi(\widetilde{\pi}_{t})+\eta\nabla_{\pi}v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi}^{f}(\rho)|_{\pi=\pi_{t}}\,,

(15)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\textstyle\pi_{t+1}

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\argmin_{\pi}}\{\Phi(\pi)-\langle\nabla\Phi(\widetilde{\pi}_{t+1}),\pi-\widetilde{\pi}_{t+1}\rangle\}\,.

where
\pi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})^{|\mathcal{S}|}
is a policy. Denoting
\theta_{t}=\nabla\Phi(\widetilde{\pi}_{t})
, one obtains updates that resemble (
12
) (without
\mathcal{T}_{\tau_{\lambda}}
), with one key difference: the gradient in (
15
) is taken with respect to the policy
\pi
whereas in (
12
) it is computed w.r.t the ”dual” parameter
\theta
(in the mirror descent terminology). Moreover, the update (
15
) can be expressed as, by the chain rule,
\smash{\textstyle\theta_{t+1}=\theta_{t}+\eta[\frac{\partial\pi_{\theta}^{f}}{\partial\theta}\big|_{\theta=\theta_{t}}]^{-1}\nabla_{\theta}J^{f}(\theta_{t})}
, which have an additional preconditioning term given by the inverse of the policy Jacobian.
See
Appendix

H
for more details.

Next, we bound the bias and variance of the gradient estimator (a proof is provided in
Section

E.1
).

Lemma 4.2
.

Assume that, for some
\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}>0
,
f
satisfy
\textbf{A}_{f}(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})
. For any parameter
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{S}||\mathcal{A}|}
, we have

\displaystyle\!\left\|\smash{\mathrm{g}^{f}(\theta)-\tfrac{\partial v_{\hskip 0.51534pt\theta}^{f}(\rho)}{\partial\theta}}\right\|_{2}\!\leq\!\beta_{f},\kern 5.0pt\mathbb{E}_{Z}\big[\left\|\smash{\mathrm{g}^{f}(\theta)-\mathrm{g}_{Z}^{f}(\theta)}\right\|_{2}^{2}\big]\leq\tfrac{\sigma_{f}^{2}}{B}\kern 5.0pt,

where
Z\sim[\nu(\theta)]^{\otimes B}
, and where

\displaystyle\textstyle\sigma_{f}^{2}

\displaystyle\textstyle:=\tfrac{12}{(1-\gamma)^{4}}\left[\omega_{f}^{3}+\lambda^{2}\gamma^{2}\omega_{f}^{3}\mathrm{d}_{f}^{2}+\lambda^{2}(1-\gamma)^{2}\omega_{f}^{2}\mathrm{y}_{f}^{2}\right]\kern 5.0pt,

\displaystyle\textstyle\beta_{f}

\displaystyle\textstyle:=\tfrac{2\gamma^{H}(H+1)}{(1-\gamma)^{2}}\omega_{f}\left[2+2\lambda\mathrm{d}_{f}+\lambda(1-\gamma)\mathrm{y}_{f}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

Convergence analysis.

We now present our main result for this section, which gives a convergence rate for
f
-PG

with explicit constants
for the regularized problem.
This result is based on the regularity properties of the regularized value function, which we developed in
Section

3
.

Theorem 4.3
.

Assume that, for some
\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}>0
,
f
and
\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}
satisfy
\textbf{A}_{f}(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})
and
\textbf{P}(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})
, and that
\rho
satisfies
\textbf{A}_{\rho}
.
Fix
\eta\leq 1/(2L_{f})
, and
\lambda
and
\tau_{\lambda}
as in
Corollary

3.6
.
Then, for any
t\geq 0
, the iterates of
f
-PG
satisfy

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathbb{E}\left[\Delta_{t}\right]\textstyle\leq\left(1-\frac{\eta\underline{\mu}_{f}}{4}\right)^{t}\Delta_{0}+\frac{6\eta\sigma_{f}^{2}}{B\underline{\mu}_{f}}+\frac{6\beta_{f}^{2}}{\underline{\mu}_{f}}\kern 5.0pt,

where
\Delta_{t}=v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}^{f}(\rho)-v_{\hskip 0.57367pt{\theta_{t}}}^{f}(\rho)
, and the expressions of
\sigma_{f}^{2}
and
\beta_{f}^{2}
are given respectively in
Corollary

3.6
and
Lemma

4.2
.

We provide a proof of this result in
Section

E.2
.
A crucial feature of this theorem is that it is
explicit
, as all the terms that appear can be expressed using problem-dependent constants.

This allows us to derive the following sample complexity result for optimizing the regularized value.

Corollary 4.4
.

Let
\epsilon>0
.
Under assumptions of
Theorem

4.3
, the final iterate of
f
-PG
satisfies
\mathbb{E}[v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}^{f}(\rho)-v_{\hskip 0.57367pt{\theta_{T}}}^{f}(\rho)]\leq\epsilon
with

\displaystyle\textstyle T\!\lesssim\max\Big(\frac{L_{f}}{\underline{\mu}_{f}},\frac{\sigma_{f}^{2}}{\epsilon B\underline{\mu}_{f}^{2}}\Big)\log\Big(\frac{v_{\hskip 0.51534pt\star}^{f}(\rho)-v_{\hskip 0.51534pt{\theta_{0}}}^{f}(\rho)}{\epsilon}\Big)\,,

H\lesssim(1-\gamma)^{2}\cdot\log(1/\underline{\mu}_{f})
, and
\eta\lesssim\min(L_{f}^{-1},\epsilon B\underline{\mu}_{f}\cdot\sigma_{f}^{-2})
.

Importantly,
Corollary

4.4
shows that
f
-PG
achieves convergence rates comparable to stochastic gradient ascent in the strongly convex regime:
O(\kappa\log(1/\epsilon))
in the low-variance setting, where
\kappa>0
denotes a condition number, and
O((1/\epsilon)\log(1/\epsilon))
in general. Notably, this is the first result to establish such guarantees even for the KL-regularized policy gradient with softmax parameterization.

Convergence for unregularized objective.

A natural question is then how to compare different choices of regularizers, since each method optimizes a distinct regularized objective. By appropriately tuning the temperature
\lambda
, we recover the final sample complexity bound for the
unregularized problem
, which is given in the corollary below. A precise statement and proof are provided in
Corollary

E.10
.

Corollary 4.5
.

Define
\mathrm{c}_{f}=\min(1/\mathrm{d}_{f},1/\mathrm{y}_{f},1)
. Let
\smash{0\!<\!\epsilon\!<\!(1\!-\!\gamma)^{-3}\!\rho_{\min}^{-1}\!\min(\left|f^{\prime}(\iota_{f})\right|^{-1}\!\!\!\!,\left|f^{\prime}(\frac{1}{2})\right|^{-1}\!\!\!\!\!,\left|f^{\prime}(\tfrac{\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}}{2})\right|^{-1})}
and set
\lambda={(1-\gamma)\epsilon}/{4}\cdot\mathrm{c}_{f}
.
Under assumptions of
Theorem

4.3
, the final iterate of
f
-PG
satisfies
\mathbb{E}\left[v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}(\rho)-v_{\hskip 0.57367pt{\theta_{T}}}(\rho)\right]\leq\epsilon
with

\displaystyle T\lesssim\frac{(f^{\star})^{\prime\prime}(\frac{-1}{\epsilon\mathrm{c}_{f}(1-\gamma)^{3}\rho_{\min}})^{-4}}{\epsilon^{3}B(1-\gamma)^{8}\rho_{\min}^{4}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{4}}\log\Big(\frac{6(v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}^{f}(\rho)-v_{\hskip 0.57367pt{\theta_{0}}}^{f}(\rho))}{\epsilon}\Big)\kern 5.0pt,

H\lesssim(1-\gamma)^{-2}\cdot\log(1/\underline{\mu}_{f})
, and
\eta\lesssim a\wedge b
with
a=(1-\gamma)^{3}
and
b=\epsilon^{2}(f^{\star})^{\prime\prime}(\tfrac{-1}{\epsilon\mathrm{c}_{f}(1-\gamma)^{6}\rho_{\min}})^{2}(1-\gamma)^{3}B\rho_{\min}^{2}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{2}
, where
f^{\star}
is a convex conjugate of
f
.

This corollary shows that the convergence rate to the unregularised optimum is primarily controlled by the asymptotic behaviour of the second derivative of the convex conjugate
(f^{\star})^{\prime\prime}
. As the target precision
\varepsilon\to 0
, divergences for which
(f^{\star})^{\prime\prime}
grows faster yield better conditioning, which in turn results in faster convergence.

(b)

\sigma^{2}=0.5

:

Learning curves for
Noisy CartPole
(top row) and
DeepSea
(bottom row) under different choices of the Tsallis parameter
\alpha
.
For
Noisy CartPole
, we report the standard
unnoised

CartPole
environment (a) and reward–noisy variants with increasing noise levels (b–d).
For
DeepSea
, we consider grid sizes
L\in\{20,30,40,50\}
(e–h).
Each curve corresponds to the best temperature and step-size for a given
\alpha
, and shaded regions indicate
\pm
one standard error over
25
seeds.
On
Noisy CartPole
, values
\alpha<1
consistently improve performance over the PPO baseline in the standard and low-noise settings, with the gap increasing as the reward noise grows.
On
DeepSea
, the improvement over the
PPO
baseline becomes more pronounced with increasing
L
, where
\alpha=0.7
achieves the highest returns and the fastest learning.

Sample complexity for specific choices of
\boldsymbol{f}
.

We now provide a more complete interpretation of these results by stating sample complexity bounds for specific choices of
f
.

Corollary 4.6

(Complexity for Softmax-Entropy)
.

Let
f
be the Kullback-Leibler divergence generator.
Let
\epsilon>0
.
Under the choice of
\eta,\lambda,H,\tau_{\lambda},
and
T
of
Corollary

4.5
, the final iterate of
f
-PG
achieves
\mathbb{E}[v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}(\rho)-v_{\hskip 0.57367pt{\theta_{T}}}(\rho)]\leq\epsilon
in

TBH\lesssim\frac{|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|^{3}}{\epsilon^{4}(1-\gamma)^{12}\rho_{\min}^{5}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{4}}\exp(\frac{|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|}{\epsilon(1-\gamma)^{3}\rho_{\min}})
samples.

This corollary shows that the number of samples required by the softmax policy gradient method is exponential in
1/(1-\gamma)
.
This is in line with recent work on vanilla softmax policy gradient, which demonstrated that the number of steps is at least exponential in
1/(1-\gamma)

[
15
]
.

Corollary 4.7

(Complexity for
\alpha
-Tsallis SoftArgmax with
\alpha
-Tsallis regularization)
.

Let
f
be the
\alpha
-Csiszár–Cressie–Read divergence generator for
\alpha\in(0,1)
(see
Table

2
for its expression).
Let
\epsilon>0
.
Under the choice of
\eta,\lambda,H,\tau_{\lambda},
and
T
of
Corollary

4.5
, the last iterate of
f
-PG
achieves
\mathbb{E}[v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}(\rho)-v_{\hskip 0.57367pt{\theta_{T}}}(\rho)]\leq\epsilon
in a number of samples

\displaystyle TBH\lesssim\frac{|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|^{3}(1+\frac{(1-\alpha)|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|}{\epsilon\alpha^{2}(1-\gamma)^{3}\rho_{\min}})^{4+\frac{4}{1-\alpha}}}{\epsilon^{4}\alpha^{6}(1-\gamma)^{12}\rho_{\min}^{5}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{4+7(1-\alpha)}}.

We give detailed versions and prove these corollaries in
Appendix

F
.
These corollaries show that Tsallis SoftArgmax parameterization with coupled regularisation allows for faster learning, reducing the dependency on
(1-\gamma)^{-1}
from exponential in
Corollary

4.6
to polynomial in
Corollary

4.7
. Next, we approximate the choice of
\alpha
that achieves the fastest convergence (according to our bounds).

Corollary 4.8
.

Assume the conditions of
Corollary

4.7
hold. The value of
\alpha
that minimizes the sample complexity in
Corollary

4.7
is given by
\alpha^{\star}(\epsilon)={11}/{(2\log(1/\epsilon))}\!+\!o\left(1/{\log(1/\epsilon)}\right)
. Moreover, for
\epsilon
sufficiently small, choosing
\alpha=\alpha^{\star}(\epsilon)
yields a sample complexity
\epsilon^{-12}\mathrm{poly}(1/(1-\gamma),1/\rho_{\min},1/\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})
up to logarithmic factors.

We prove this corollary in
Appendix

F
.
These results show that the best choice of
\alpha
is not
\alpha=0
nor
\alpha=1
, but depends on the desired precision level.
This corroborates results from the bandit literature
[
37
]
, and gives strong evidence that Tsallis-SoftArgmax with coupled regularization has the potential to accelerate RL algorithms.
It also highlights the strength of our framework: one can choose, among multiple parameterizations, the one that is best suited for the problem at hand.

5
Experiments

In this section, we demonstrate the generalizability of our framework by showing that our class of parameterizations, with its coupled regularization, can be readily integrated into modern on-policy reinforcement learning algorithms. For this purpose, we introduce and evaluate
\alpha
-
Tsallis PPO
, a simple yet principled extension of Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO;
32
)
1
1

1

Additional experiments on the exact
f
-PG
algorithm and on the methods listed in
Table

1
are provided in
Appendix

J
.
.
Our approach is obtained by replacing both the policy parameterization and the entropy regularization in PPO with their Tsallis counterparts (see
Appendix

J
for full experimental details). We compare the performance of
\alpha
-
Tsallis PPO
against the standard
PPO
baseline
[
32
]
on two families of environments that we describe below.

Noisy CartPole
[
25
]
.
This environment is a variant of the classic
CartPole
control task in which additive noise is injected into the reward signal.
The underlying dynamics is unchanged: at each time step, the agent applies a left or right force to a cart in order to keep an inverted pendulum balanced, receiving a base reward of
+1
for each step the pole remains upright, and the episode terminates when stability is lost or after a time limit. However, the reward returned by the environment is perturbed as
\tilde{r}_{t}=r_{t}+\sigma\,\xi_{t},\kern 5.0pt\xi_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1),

where
\sigma>0
controls the noise level.
This preserves the dynamics and optimal policy, but increases the variance of observed returns.

DeepSea
[
26
]
.

DeepSea
is an RL environment designed to study deep exploration under sparse rewards.
The environment is a directed grid of size
L\times L
. The agent starts in the top-left corner
(0,0)
and, at each step, moves downward while choosing between two actions that shift agent’s position either left or right.
Thus, each episode lasts exactly
L
steps and corresponds to selecting a binary action sequence of length
L
, which defines a unique path through the grid. Only a single trajectory, the one that selects the hidden
correct
(right) action at every depth reaches the rewarding terminal state at
(L{-}1,L{-}1)
.
However, selecting the right action is not free: every time the agent moves right, it incurs a small movement cost
0.01/L
.

Problem-adaptive couplings yield better performance.

Figure

2
illustrates that no single choice of
\alpha
is uniformly optimal, and that different tasks favor different couplings of parameterization and regularization.
On
Noisy CartPole
, the standard setting (
Figure

2(a)
) and the mildly noisy variant (
Figure

2(b)
) show a small but systematic advantage for
\alpha<1
over the PPO baseline, which becomes more pronounced as the reward noise increases (
Figures

2(c)
and
2(d)
).
By contrast, on
DeepSea
, where performance depends on discovering a single sparse-reward trajectory, the hardest instances (
Figures

2(g)
and
2(h)
) favor an intermediate value
\alpha\!=\!0.7
. These observations suggest that highly noisy environments and deep exploration problems may benefit from different regions of the Tsallis family, supporting the need for a tunable parameterization–regularization pair.

6
Conclusion

We proposed a new class of policy parameterizations based on operators induced by
f
-divergences. Equipped with a matching
f
-divergence regularizer, this framework generalizes the classical softmax–entropy pairing and allows flexible alternative parameterizations. Using Tsallis divergence instead of Shannon entropy, we showed that the resulting algorithm yields polynomial, rather than exponential, convergence guarantees for the unregularized RL problem. Empirically, this choice leads to improved performance in exploration-heavy and noisy environments. An important direction for future work is to extend these guarantees for adversarial MDPs, where Tsallis regularization has already proven effective in the bandit setting
[
37
]
.

\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})

Set of probability measures over
\mathcal{A}

Section

2

\operatorname{D}^{f}(p\|q)

f
-divergence between two probability measures
p
and
q

(
16
)

f
-Divergence.

Let
f\colon(0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
be a strictly convex
generator
with
f(1)=0
. Its
adjoint
(or reverse generator) is
f_{\dagger}(u):=u\,f(1/u)
,
u>0
, which is convex, strictly convex if
f
is, and satisfies
f_{\dagger}(1)=0
. Boundary conventions are

f(0):=\lim_{u\downarrow 0}f(u)\in(-\infty,\infty]
and
f_{\dagger}(0):=\lim_{u\downarrow 0}f_{\dagger}(u)=\lim_{t\uparrow\infty}f(t)/t\in(-\infty,\infty]
.
For
p,q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})
over finite
\mathcal{A}
, the
f
–divergence is

\displaystyle\operatorname{D}^{f}(p\|q)\;:=\;

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}:q(a)>0}q(a)f(p(a)/q(a))+f_{\dagger}(0)\cdot\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}:q(a)=0}p(a),

(16)

with conventions:
q(a)f(0)
if
q(a)>0,p(a)=0
, and
0
if
p(a)=q(a)=0

[
29
,
6
,
16
]
.

f
–divergences satisfy
\operatorname{D}^{f}(p\|q)\in[0,\infty]
, are jointly convex
[
6
]
, vanish iff
p=q
, and are not symmetric (
\operatorname{D}^{f_{\dagger}}(p\|q)=\operatorname{D}^{f}(p\|q)
).

Distribution of the state-action sequence.

The state–action sequence
(S_{t},A_{t})_{t\geq 0}
defines a stochastic process on the canonical space
(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A})^{\mathbb{N}}
.
For any initial state
s_{0}\in\mathcal{S}
, we denote by
\mathbb{P}_{s_{0}}^{\pi}
the law of this process.
That is, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and any subset
B\subset(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A})^{n}
,

\mathbb{P}_{s_{0}}^{\pi}(B)=\sum_{(a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1})\in\mathcal{A}^{n}}\;\sum_{(s_{1},\dots,s_{n-1})\in\mathcal{S}^{n-1}}\mathbbm{1}_{B}\!\bigl((s_{0},a_{0}),\dots,(s_{n-1},a_{n-1})\bigr)\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\pi(a_{i}\mid s_{i})\,\mathsf{P}(s_{i+1}\mid s_{i},a_{i}),

with the convention
s_{0}
is the given initial state.
We denote by
\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}}^{\pi}
the corresponding expectation operator.
In particular, the state sequence
(s_{t})_{t\geq 0}
defines a Markov reward process (Section 2.1.6 in
[
28
]
) with transition kernel

\mathsf{P}_{\pi}(s^{\prime}\mid s)=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mathsf{P}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a)\,\pi(a\mid s)\kern 5.0pt.

Norms.

For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we define the norms

\displaystyle\|x\|_{\infty}=\max_{i\in\{1,\dots,d\}}|x_{i}|\kern 5.0pt,\quad\|x\|_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}|x_{i}|\kern 5.0pt,\quad\|x\|_{2}=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}|x_{i}|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\kern 5.0pt.

For a
d\times d
matrix
M
, we denote by
\|M\|_{\infty}
, and
\|M\|_{2}
respectively the max
row
sum, and the spectral norm:

\|M\|_{\infty}=\sup_{x\neq 0}\{\left\|Mx\right\|_{\infty}/\left\|x\right\|_{\infty}\}=\sup_{i\in\{1,\dots,d\}}\sum_{j=1}^{d}|M_{i,j}|\kern 5.0pt,\quad\|M\|_{2}=\sup_{x\neq 0}\{\left\|Mx\right\|_{2}/\left\|x\right\|_{2}\}\kern 5.0pt.

(17)

Recall that, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\|Mx\|_{\infty}\leq\|M\|_{\infty}\|x\|_{\infty}
and
\|Mx\|_{2}\leq\|M\|_{2}\|x\|_{2}
.

Functional and matrix forms.

For notational convenience, we also view
\mathsf{P}
as a
(|\mathcal{S}|\cdot|\mathcal{A}|)\times|\mathcal{S}|
matrix with entries
\mathsf{P}_{(s,a),s^{\prime}}=\mathsf{P}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a)
. Similarly,
v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi}^{f}
is a vector of size
|\mathcal{S}|
and
q_{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi}^{f}
a vector of size
|\mathcal{S}|\times|\mathcal{A}|
. Finally, we identify the parameter
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}
with its matrix representation
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{S}||\mathcal{A}|}
, indexed by
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
. This slight abuse of notation allows us to conveniently switch between functional and matrix views.

Appendix B
Smoothness of the objective

In this section, we establish the smoothness of the regularized value function
v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}^{f}:=v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi_{\theta}}^{f}(\rho)
with respect to the parameter
\theta
. As a first step, we show that the policy
\pi_{\theta}^{f}
is smooth under suitable assumptions on the divergence generator
f
and compute its first and second derivatives. To do so, we start by studying the properties of the soft-
f
-argmax operator and then apply the obtained results to derive properties of the policy
\pi_{\theta}^{f}
.

B.1
Properties of the soft-
f
-argmax

In this Section, we compute the derivative of
\nu_{x}^{f}(\cdot):=\operatorname{f-softargmax}(x,\nu_{\operatorname{ref}})
and
\operatorname{x-softmax}(\cdot):=\operatorname{f-softmax}(x,\nu_{\operatorname{ref}})
, where

\displaystyle\operatorname{f-softmax}(x,\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}):=\max_{\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})}\left\{\langle\nu,x\rangle-\operatorname{D}^{f}(\nu\|\nu_{\operatorname{ref}})\right\}\kern 5.0pt.

(18)

The function
\operatorname{x-softmax}(\cdot)
is the Fenchel–Legendre transform of

\operatorname{D}^{f}(\cdot\|\nu_{\operatorname{ref}})
, and the results in this section are therefore
standard results from convex analysis, statements of which can be found in various forms in

[
10
,
22
,
9
,
30
]
.

In this section, we fix a reference probability distribution
\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})
such that for any
a\in\mathcal{A}
, we have
\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)>\underline{\nu_{\text{ref}}}
for some
\min_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)>\underline{\nu_{\text{ref}}}>0
. For a given
x\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{A}|}
, we define

\displaystyle\nu_{x}^{f}(\cdot):=\operatorname{f-softargmax}(x,\nu_{\operatorname{ref}})=\argmax_{\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})}\left\{\langle\nu,x\rangle-\operatorname{D}^{f}(\nu\|\nu_{\operatorname{ref}})\right\}\kern 5.0pt.

(19)

The following result is a simplified version of
[
30
, Proposition 1]
.
For the sake of completeness, we provide a full proof.

Lemma B.1
.

Assume that
f
is strictly convex on
[0,1/\underline{\nu_{\text{ref}}}]
, differentiable on
(0,1/\underline{\nu_{\text{ref}}})
, with
\lim_{x\downarrow 0^{+}}f^{\prime}(u)=-\infty
, for some
\underline{\nu_{\text{ref}}}>0
. Let
\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}
be a policy such that
\min_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)>\underline{\nu_{\text{ref}}}
.
For any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{A}|}
and
a\in\mathcal{A}
, we have
0<\nu_{x}^{f}(a)
.
Moreover, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{A}|}
, there exists a unique
\mu_{x}\in I_{\underline{\nu_{\text{ref}}}}(x)
, where

I_{\underline{\nu_{\text{ref}}}}(x):=(\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}x(a)-f^{\prime}(1/\underline{\nu_{\text{ref}}}),\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}x(a)-f^{\prime}(1)),

(20)

such that

\nu_{x}^{f}(a)=\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)[f^{\prime}]^{-1}(x(a)-\mu_{x})\,.

(21)

Moreover,
\mu_{x}\in\mathbb{R}
is the unique root of the equation

F(x,\mu):=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)[f^{\prime}]^{-1}(x(a)-\mu)-1=0\,\quad\text{ for $\mu\in I_{\underline{\nu_{\text{ref}}}}(x)$}.

(22)

Proof.

Under the stated assumption, the map
f^{\prime}
is strictly increasing on
(0,1/\underline{\nu_{\text{ref}}}]
, hence injective; therefore it is invertible
onto its image
. Moreover,

\operatorname{Dom}\big([f^{\prime}]^{-1}\big)=f^{\prime}\big((0,1/\underline{\nu_{\text{ref}}}]\big)=\bigl(-\infty,\;f^{\prime}(1/\underline{\nu_{\text{ref}}})\bigr],

(23)

which is an interval and the function
[f^{\prime}]^{-1}
is strictly increasing.

Fix
x\in\mathcal{A}
, and
(a,b)\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}
.
Recall from (
19
) the definition of the soft-
f
-argmax ,

\displaystyle\nu_{x}^{f}=\argmax_{\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})}\left\{\langle\nu,x\rangle-\operatorname{D}^{f}(\nu\|\nu_{\operatorname{ref}})\right\}

This is a strictly concave optimization problem over the probability simplex

\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})
so it admits a unique maximizer. We now characterize the maximizer via the KKT conditions.
Introduce multipliers
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
for the equality constraint

\sum_{c\in\mathcal{A}}\nu(c)=1
, and for every
c\in\mathcal{A}
,
\lambda(c)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}

for the non-negativity constraints
\nu(c)\geq 0
.
The Lagrangian reads

\displaystyle L(\nu,\mu,\{\lambda(c)\}_{c\in\mathcal{A}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{c\in\mathcal{A}}\nu(c)\,x(c)-\operatorname{D}^{f}(\nu\|\nu_{\operatorname{ref}})+\mu\Bigl(1-\sum_{c\in\mathcal{A}}\nu(c)\Bigr)-\sum_{c\in\mathcal{A}}\lambda(c)\,\nu(c)\kern 5.0pt.

By the differentiability of
f
, differentiating the Lagrangian with respect to
\nu(a)
gives

\displaystyle\frac{\partial L}{\partial\nu(a)}=x(a)-f^{\prime}\left(\frac{\nu(a)}{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)}\right)-\mu-\lambda(a)\kern 5.0pt.

(24)

At the optimum
(\nu_{x}^{f},\mu_{x},\{\lambda_{x}(c)\}_{c\in\mathcal{A}})
, the KKT conditions yield:

\displaystyle\nu_{x}^{f}(c)\,\lambda_{x}(c)

\displaystyle=0,\qquad\forall c\in\mathcal{A},

(25)

\displaystyle x(c)-f^{\prime}\left(\frac{\nu_{x}^{f}(c)}{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(c)}\right)-\mu_{x}-\lambda_{x}(c)

\displaystyle=0,\qquad\forall c\in\mathcal{A}.

(26)

Under the stated assumptions,
\lim_{x\to 0^{+}}f^{\prime}(x)=-\infty
.
Hence, if
\nu_{x}^{f}(c)=0
for some
c
,
the stationarity condition (
26
) cannot hold with finite multipliers.
Therefore
\nu_{x}^{f}(c)>0
for all
c\in\mathcal{A}
,
which by (
25
) implies
\lambda_{x}(c)=0
. Thus, for each
c\in\mathcal{A}
the stationarity condition reduces to

x(c)-f^{\prime}\left(\frac{\nu_{x}^{f}(c)}{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(c)}\right)=\mu_{x}\kern 5.0pt.

(27)

Note also that (
27
) also implies that for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
,
x(a)-\mu_{x}\in\operatorname{Dom}(f^{\prime})
, which implies, using (
23
) that
\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}x(a)-f^{\prime}(1/\underline{\nu_{\text{ref}}})\leq\mu_{x}
. Together with (
27
), this shows (
21
). Note that
\mu_{x}
is a root of (
22
), since using (
21
),

\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)[f^{\prime}]^{-1}(x(a)-\mu_{x})=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\nu_{x}^{f}(a)=1.

Because
[f^{\prime}]^{-1}
is strictly increasing on
(-\infty,f^{\prime}(1/\underline{\nu_{\text{ref}}})]
, for each
x\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{A}|}
, the function
\mu\mapsto F(x,\mu)
(see (
22
)) is strictly decreasing on
(-\infty,f^{\prime}(1/\underline{\nu_{\text{ref}}})]
. Strict monotonicity gives the uniqueness of
\mu_{x}
.
Note finally that if
\mu>\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}x(a)-f^{\prime}(1)
, then
x(a)-\mu<f^{\prime}(1)
, and since
[f^{\prime}]^{-1}
is strictly increasing,
[f^{\prime}]^{-1}(x(a)-\mu)<1
, showing that
F(x,\mu)<0
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Remark B.2

Let
f:(0,\tau)\to\mathbb{R}
be a strictly convex and differentiable on
(0,\tau)
,
\operatorname{Dom}(f^{\prime})=(0,\tau)
, and
f^{\prime}
is strictly increasing and continuous on
(0,\tau)
. Let
\alpha:=\lim_{x\rightarrow 0}f^{\prime}(x)\in[-\infty,+\infty)
and

\beta:=\lim_{x\rightarrow\tau}f^{\prime}(x)\in(-\infty,+\infty]
. Then

f^{\prime}\bigl((0,\tau)\bigr)=(\alpha,\beta)
, i.e.,
f^{\prime}:(0,\tau)\to(\alpha,\beta)
is a strictly increasing bijection (hence admits a continuous inverse
[f^{\prime}]^{-1}:(\alpha,\beta)\to(0,\tau)
). Define the convex conjugate of
f
,

\displaystyle f^{*}(y):=\sup_{x\in(0,\tau)}\{xy-f(x)\}\kern 5.0pt.

(28)

The two following properties hold,

(i)

For every
y\in(\alpha,\beta)
, the supremum is attained at a unique point

x=(f^{\prime})^{-1}(y)
.

(ii)

\operatorname{Dom}(f^{*})\subseteq[\alpha,\beta]
(with the convention that an infinite
endpoint is excluded). Specifically,
\operatorname{Dom}(f^{*})\cap(\alpha,\beta)=(\alpha,\beta)
,
and
f^{*}(y)=+\infty
for
y\notin[\alpha,\beta]
. At an endpoint
y=\alpha

(resp.
y=\beta
), if finite,
f^{*}(y)=\lim_{x\downarrow 0}(yx-f(x))

(resp.
\lim_{x\uparrow\tau}(yx-f(x))
), so
f^{*}(\alpha)
(resp.
f^{*}(\beta)
)
is finite iff the corresponding one–sided limit is finite.

On the open interval
(\alpha,\beta)
, the function
f^{*}
is differentiable and

(f^{*})^{\prime}(y)=[f^{\prime}]^{-1}(y),\qquad y\in(\alpha,\beta),

We retrieve the statement in Proposition 1 of
[
30
]
by replacing
[f^{\prime}]^{-1}
by
(f^{*})^{\prime}
. In most examples, there is no need to resort to the convex conjugate to compute the inverse.

Lemma B.3
.

Assume, in addition to
Lemma

B.1
, that
f
is two-times continuously differentiable on
(0,\underline{\nu_{\text{ref}}}
. Then, the function
x\mapsto\mu_{x}
is continuously differentiable on
\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{A}|}
, and for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
,

\frac{\partial}{\partial x(a)}\mu_{x}=-\frac{\partial}{\partial x(a)}F(x,\mu_{x})/\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}F(x,\mu_{x})

(29)

Proof.

The function
(x,\mu)\mapsto F(x,\mu)
is two-times continuously differentiable on the open set

U:=\{(x,\mu):x\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{A}|},\mu\in I_{\underline{\nu_{\text{ref}}}}(x)\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{A}|}\times\mathbb{R}
. Let
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{A}|}
. Since
[f^{\prime}]^{-1}
is strictly increasing,

\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}F(x,\mu_{x})=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)\frac{1}{f^{\prime\prime}([f^{\prime}]^{-1}(x_{0}(a)-\mu_{x_{0}}))}>0.

Hence, we may apply the implicit function theorem, which shows that there exists an open neighborhood
V_{x_{0}}
and a unique function
x\mapsto\mu_{x}
on
V_{x_{0}}
, such that for all
x\in V_{x_{0}}
,
F(x,\mu_{x})=0
and (
29
) holds.
∎

We now introduce some compact notations that will be used throughout the sequel.
For any
a\in\mathcal{A}
, define the first three derivatives of
f
evaluated at the probability ratio
\nu_{x}^{f}(a)/\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)
:

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{x}(a):=f^{\prime}\left(\frac{\nu_{x}^{f}(a)}{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)}\right)\kern 5.0pt,\quad f^{\prime\prime}_{x}(a):=f^{\prime\prime}\left(\frac{\nu_{x}^{f}(a)}{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)}\right)\kern 5.0pt,\quad f^{\prime\prime\prime}_{x}(a):=f^{\prime\prime\prime}\left(\frac{\nu_{x}^{f}(a)}{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

(30)

In addition, we introduce the quantities

\displaystyle\operatorname{W}_{x}^{f}\;:=\;\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)}{f^{\prime\prime}_{x}(a)}\kern 5.0pt.

(31)

Importantly, as
f
is strictly convex, its second derivative is strictly positive, making the preceding quantity well-defined. For any
a\in\mathcal{A}
, we also define the following normalized weights

\displaystyle\operatorname{w}_{x}^{f}(a)=\frac{1}{\operatorname{W}_{x}^{f}}\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)}{f^{\prime\prime}_{x}(a)}\kern 5.0pt.

(32)

Lemma B.4
.

Assume
\textbf{A}_{f}(\underline{\nu_{\text{ref}}})
. Then the soft-
f
-argmax
\nu_{x}^{f}
is twice continuously differentiable with respect to
x
. Moreover, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{A}|}
, and
(a,b)\in\mathcal{A}^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\operatorname{W}_{x}^{f}}\cdot\frac{\partial\nu_{x}^{f}(a)}{\partial x(b)}=\mathsf{1}_{b}(a)\operatorname{w}_{x}^{f}(a)-\operatorname{w}_{x}^{f}(a)\operatorname{w}_{x}^{f}(b)\kern 5.0pt,

In addition, for any
(a,b,c)\in\mathcal{A}^{3}
, the second derivative satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\operatorname{W}_{x}^{f}}\cdot\frac{\partial\nu_{x}^{f}(a)}{\partial x(b)\partial x(c)}

\displaystyle=-\mathsf{1}_{b}(a)\mathsf{1}_{c}(a)\frac{f_{x}^{\prime\prime\prime}\left(a\right)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(a)^{2}}\cdot\operatorname{w}_{x}^{f}(a)+\mathsf{1}_{c}(b)\operatorname{w}_{x}^{f}(a)\operatorname{w}_{x}^{f}(b)\frac{f_{x}^{\prime\prime\prime}\left(b\right)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}\left(b\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\mathsf{1}_{b}(a)\operatorname{w}_{x}^{f}(a)\operatorname{w}_{x}^{f}(c)\frac{f_{x}^{\prime\prime\prime}\left(a\right)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}\left(a\right)^{2}}+\mathsf{1}_{c}(a)\operatorname{w}_{x}^{f}(a)\operatorname{w}_{x}^{f}(b)\frac{f_{x}^{\prime\prime\prime}\left(a\right)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}\left(a\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad-\operatorname{w}_{x}^{f}(a)\cdot\operatorname{w}_{x}^{f}(b)\cdot\operatorname{w}_{x}^{f}(c)\cdot\left[\frac{f_{x}^{\prime\prime\prime}(a)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(a)^{2}}+\frac{f_{x}^{\prime\prime\prime}(b)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(b)^{2}}+\frac{f_{x}^{\prime\prime\prime}(c)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(c)^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\operatorname{w}_{x}^{f}(a)\cdot\operatorname{w}_{x}^{f}(b)\cdot\operatorname{w}_{x}^{f}(c)\cdot\sum_{d\in\mathcal{A}}\operatorname{w}_{x}^{f}(d)\cdot\frac{f_{x}^{\prime\prime\prime}\left(d\right)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}\left(d\right)^{2}}\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

Fix
x\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{A}|}
and
(a,b,c)\in\mathcal{A}^{3}
. Define

\displaystyle F(x;\mu)=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)[f^{\prime}]^{-1}(x(a)-\mu_{x})-1\kern 5.0pt.

First derivative.

Importantly, using
Lemma

B.1
we have that

\displaystyle F(x;\mu_{x})=0\kern 5.0pt.

Differentiating the previous identity with respect to
x(b)
, yields

\displaystyle\frac{\partial F(x;\mu_{x})}{\partial x(b)}=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)\frac{1}{f^{\prime\prime}([f^{\prime}]^{-1}(x(a)-\mu_{x}))}\left(\mathsf{1}_{b}(a)-\frac{\partial\mu_{x}}{\partial x(b)}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

where we used that the derivative of
[f^{\prime}]^{-1}
is
1/f^{\prime\prime}([f^{\prime}]^{-1})
. Next, using from
Lemma

B.1
that
\nu_{x}^{f}(a)=\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)[f^{\prime}]^{-1}(x(a)-\mu_{x})
yields

\displaystyle\frac{\partial F(x;\mu_{x})}{\partial x(b)}=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)\frac{1}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(a)}\left(\mathsf{1}_{b}(a)-\frac{\partial\mu_{x}}{\partial x(b)}\right)=0\kern 5.0pt,

where
f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(a)
is defined in (
30
). This implies

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mu_{x}}{\partial x(b)}=\operatorname{w}_{x}^{f}(b)\kern 5.0pt,

(33)

where
\operatorname{w}_{x}^{f}(b)
is defined in (
32
). Now that we have computed the derivative of the normalization factor
\mu_{x}
, we can compute the derivative of the policy. Starting from
Lemma

B.1
, we have that

\nu_{x}^{f}(a)=\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)[f^{\prime}]^{-1}(x(a)-\mu_{x})\kern 5.0pt.

Differentiating the previous identity with respect to
x(b)
, yields

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\nu_{x}^{f}(a)}{\partial x(b)}=\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(a)}\left[\mathsf{1}_{b}(a)-\frac{\partial\mu_{x}}{\partial x(b)}\right]=\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(a)}\left[\mathsf{1}_{b}(a)-\operatorname{w}_{x}^{f}(b)\right]\kern 5.0pt,

where in the last identity, we used the expression of the derivative of
\mu_{x}
given in (
33
). Finally, using the definition of
\operatorname{W}_{x}^{f}
given in (
31
) gives

\displaystyle\boxed{\frac{\partial\nu_{x}^{f}(a)}{\partial x(b)}=\mathsf{1}_{b}(a)\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(a)}-\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(a)}\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(b)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(b)}\cdot\frac{1}{\operatorname{W}_{x}^{f}}\kern 5.0pt,}

(34)

Second derivative

From (
34
), we aim to differentiate once more with respect to
x(c)
. First, note that it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(a)}{\partial x(c)}

\displaystyle=\frac{f_{x}^{\prime\prime\prime}\left(a\right)}{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)}\cdot\frac{\partial\nu_{x}^{f}(a)}{\partial x(c)}=\mathsf{1}_{c}(a)\frac{f_{x}^{\prime\prime\prime}\left(a\right)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}\left(a\right)}-\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(c)f_{x}^{\prime\prime\prime}\left(a\right)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}\left(a\right)f_{x}^{\prime\prime}\left(c\right)}\cdot\frac{1}{\operatorname{W}_{x}^{f}}\kern 5.0pt,

(35)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\operatorname{W}_{x}^{f}}{\partial x(c)}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{d\in\mathcal{A}}\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(d)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(d)^{2}}\frac{\partial f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(d)}{\partial x(c)}=-\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(c)f_{x}^{\prime\prime\prime}(c)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(c)^{3}}+\frac{1}{\operatorname{W}_{x}^{f}}\sum_{d\in\mathcal{A}}\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(c)\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(d)f_{x}^{\prime\prime\prime}\left(d\right)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}\left(d\right)^{3}f_{x}^{\prime\prime}\left(c\right)}\kern 5.0pt,

(36)

Now computing the second derivative of
\nu_{x}^{f}
gives

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\nu_{x}^{f}(a)}{\partial x(b)\partial x(c)}=-\mathsf{1}_{b}(a)\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(a)^{2}}\frac{\partial f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(a)}{\partial x(c)}+\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(b)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(a)^{2}f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(b)}\cdot\frac{1}{\operatorname{W}_{x}^{f}}\frac{\partial f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(a)}{\partial x(c)}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(b)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(a)f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(b)^{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{\operatorname{W}_{x}^{f}}\frac{\partial f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(b)}{\partial x(c)}+\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(b)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(a)f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(b)}\cdot\frac{1}{(\operatorname{W}_{x}^{f})^{2}}\frac{\partial\operatorname{W}_{x}^{f}}{\partial x(c)}

Plugging in (
35
), and (
36
) in the preceding inequality yields

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\nu_{(}^{\theta}a)}{\partial x(b)\partial x(c)}=-\mathsf{1}_{b}(a)\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)}{f_{x}(a)^{2}}\left[\mathsf{1}_{c}(a)\frac{f_{x}^{\prime\prime\prime}\left(a\right)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}\left(a\right)}-\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(c)f_{x}^{\prime\prime\prime}\left(a\right)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}\left(a\right)f_{x}^{\prime\prime}\left(c\right)}\cdot\frac{1}{\operatorname{W}_{x}^{f}}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(b)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(a)^{2}f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(b)}\cdot\frac{1}{\operatorname{W}_{x}^{f}}\left[\mathsf{1}_{c}(a)\frac{f_{x}^{\prime\prime\prime}\left(a\right)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}\left(a\right)}-\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(c)f_{x}^{\prime\prime\prime}\left(a\right)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}\left(a\right)f_{x}^{\prime\prime}\left(c\right)}\cdot\frac{1}{\operatorname{W}_{x}^{f}}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(b)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(a)f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(b)^{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{\operatorname{W}_{x}^{f}}\left[\mathsf{1}_{c}(b)\frac{f_{x}^{\prime\prime\prime}\left(b\right)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}\left(b\right)}-\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(c)f_{x}^{\prime\prime\prime}\left(b\right)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}\left(b\right)f_{x}^{\prime\prime}\left(c\right)}\cdot\frac{1}{\operatorname{W}_{x}^{f}}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(b)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(a)f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(b)}\cdot\frac{1}{(\operatorname{W}_{x}^{f})^{2}}\left[-\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(c)f_{x}^{\prime\prime\prime}(c)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}(c)^{3}}+\frac{1}{\operatorname{W}_{x}^{f}}\sum_{d\in\mathcal{A}}\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(c)\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(d)f_{x}^{\prime\prime\prime}\left(d\right)}{f_{x}^{\prime\prime}\left(d\right)^{3}f_{x}^{\prime\prime}\left(c\right)}\right]\kern 5.0pt,

which concludes the proof.
∎
The following lemma links the gradients
\operatorname{f-softmax}
and
\operatorname{f-softargmax}
operators.

Lemma B.5
.

Assume
\textbf{A}_{f}(\underline{\nu_{\text{ref}}})
. For any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{A}|}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\operatorname{f-softmax}(x,\nu_{\operatorname{ref}})}{\partial x}=\operatorname{f-softargmax}(x,\nu_{\operatorname{ref}})\quad\text{and}\quad\left\|\frac{\partial^{2}\operatorname{f-softmax}(x,\nu_{\operatorname{ref}})]}{\partial x^{2}}\right\|_{2}\leq 2\operatorname{W}_{x}^{f}\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

For any
x\in\mathcal{A}
and
\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})
, define

\displaystyle h^{f}(x,\nu)=\langle\nu,x\rangle-\operatorname{D}^{f}(\nu\|\nu_{\operatorname{ref}})\kern 5.0pt.

(37)

Fix
b\in\mathcal{A}
and note that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial h^{f}(x,\nu)}{\partial x(b)}=\nu(b)\kern 5.0pt.

It holds that
\operatorname{f-softmax}(x)=\max_{\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})}h^{f}(x,\nu)
. As
h^{f}
is continuous in its two variables,
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})
is a compact set, and for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{A}|}
, the function
h^{f}(x,\cdot)
admits a unique optimizer in
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})
, then by Danskin’s theorem (
Lemma

I.5
)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\operatorname{f-softmax}(x,\nu_{\operatorname{ref}})}{\partial x(b)}=\frac{\partial h^{f}(x,\nu^{\star}(x))}{\partial x}=\nu^{\star}(x)\kern 5.0pt,\text{ where }\nu^{\star}(x)=\argmax_{\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})}h^{f}(x,\nu)\kern 5.0pt.

Finally, using that

\displaystyle\nu^{\star}(x)=\operatorname{f-softargmax}(x,\nu_{\operatorname{ref}})\kern 5.0pt,

establishes the first identity of the lemma. Using the fact that for any matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, we have

\left\|A\right\|_{2}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}|a_{i,j}|
,
implies

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{\partial^{2}\operatorname{f-softmax}(x,\nu_{\operatorname{ref}})]}{\partial x^{2}}\right\|_{2}\leq\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\sum_{b\in\mathcal{A}}\left|\frac{\partial\nu_{x}^{f}(a)}{\partial x(b)}\right|\leq\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\sum_{b\in\mathcal{A}}\operatorname{W}_{x}^{f}\left|\mathsf{1}_{b}(a)\operatorname{w}_{x}^{f}(a)-\operatorname{w}_{x}^{f}(a)\operatorname{w}_{x}^{f}(b)\right|

where in the last equality, we used
Lemma

B.4
. Finally, applying the triangle inequality establishes the second claim of the lemma.
∎

B.2
Specification to the
\boldsymbol{f}
-divergence generators of
Table

2

We now specify the particular forms that these quantities take under three choices of the function
f
: the KL divergence generator(
f(u)=u\log u
), the
\alpha
-Csiszár–Cressie–Read divergence generator for
0<\alpha<1
, and the Hellinger divergence generator

KL case (
f(u)=u\log u
).

Since
f^{\prime}(u)=\log u+1
,
f^{\prime\prime}(u)=1/u
, and
[f^{\prime}]^{-1}(y)=\exp(y-1)
, the soft-
f
operators specialize as follows (with base measure
\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})
):

\nu_{x}^{\text{\tiny{KL}}}(a)=\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)[f^{\prime}]^{-1}\!\bigl(x(a)-\mu_{x}\bigr)=\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)\,\exp(x(a))}{\sum_{b\in\mathcal{A}}\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(b)\,\exp(x(b))}\,,\qquad a\in\mathcal{A},

where the normalizer is

\mu_{x}\;=\;-1+\log\!\Bigl(\sum_{b\in\mathcal{A}}\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(b)\,\exp(x(b))\Bigr).

The associated softmax function is the log-partition:

\operatorname{\text{\tiny{KL}}-softmax}(x,\nu_{\operatorname{ref}})=\log\Bigl(\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)\,\exp(x(a))\Bigr).

For the curvature quantities in (
31
)–(
32
), since

f^{\prime\prime}\Bigl(\frac{\nu_{x}^{\text{\tiny{KL}}}(a)}{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)}\Bigr)=\Bigl(\frac{\nu_{x}^{\text{\tiny{KL}}}(a)}{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)}\Bigr)^{-1}=\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)}{\nu_{x}^{\text{\tiny{KL}}}(a)}\,,

we obtain

\operatorname{W}_{x}^{\text{\tiny{KL}}}=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)}{f^{\prime\prime}_{x}(a)}=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\nu_{x}^{\text{\tiny{KL}}}(a)=1

and the corresponding normalized weights are

\operatorname{w}_{x}^{\text{\tiny{KL}}}(a)=\frac{1}{\operatorname{W}_{x}^{\text{\tiny{KL}}}}\,\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)}{f^{\prime\prime}_{x}(a)}=\nu_{x}^{\text{\tiny{KL}}}(a)\,,\qquad a\in\mathcal{A}.

Tsallis-
\alpha
case (
0<\alpha<1
).

Let
f(u)=\dfrac{u^{\alpha}-\alpha u+\alpha-1}{\alpha(\alpha-1)}
, so that

f^{\prime}(u)=\frac{u^{\alpha-1}-1}{\alpha-1},\qquad f^{\prime\prime}(u)=u^{\alpha-2},\qquad[f^{\prime}]^{-1}(y)=\bigl[1+(\alpha-1)y\bigr]^{\tfrac{1}{\alpha-1}}.

The soft-
f
operators specialize (with base measure
\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})
) to

\nu_{x}^{\text{\tiny{TS}}}(a)=\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)[f^{\prime}]^{-1}(x(a)-\mu_{x})=\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)\,\bigl[1+(\alpha-1)\bigl(x(a)-\mu_{x}\bigr)\bigr]^{1/(\alpha-1)},\qquad a\in\mathcal{A},

where
\mu_{x}
is the unique normalizer satisfying the constraint

\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)\,\bigl[1+(\alpha-1)\bigl(x(a)-\mu_{x}\bigr)\bigr]^{\tfrac{1}{\alpha-1}}=1,

with the domain condition
1+(\alpha-1)\bigl(x(a)-\mu_{x}\bigr)>0
for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
.

The associated softmax is

\operatorname{\text{\tiny{TS}}-softmax}(x,\nu_{\operatorname{ref}})=\mu_{x}\;-\;\frac{1}{\alpha}\;+\;\frac{1}{\alpha}\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)\,\bigl[1+(\alpha-1)\bigl(x(a)-\mu_{x}\bigr)\bigr]^{\tfrac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}}.

For the curvature quantities in (
31
)–(
32
), set

u_{x}(a)\;:=\;\frac{\nu_{x}^{\text{\tiny{TS}}}(a)}{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)}=\bigl[1+(\alpha-1)\bigl(x(a)-\mu_{x}\bigr)\bigr]^{\tfrac{1}{\alpha-1}}.

Since
f^{\prime\prime}\bigl(u_{x}(a)\bigr)=u_{x}(a)^{\alpha-2}
, we have

\operatorname{W}_{x}^{\text{\tiny{TS}}}=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)}{f^{\prime\prime}_{x}(a)}=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)\,u_{x}(a)^{\,2-\alpha}=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)\,\bigl[1+(\alpha-1)\bigl(x(a)-\mu_{x}\bigr)\bigr]^{(2-\alpha)/(\alpha-1)},

and the corresponding normalized weights are, for
a\in\mathcal{A}
,

\operatorname{w}_{x}^{\text{\tiny{TS}}}(a)=\frac{1}{\operatorname{W}_{x}^{f}}\,\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)}{f^{\prime\prime}_{x}(a)}=\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)\,u_{x}(a)^{\,2-\alpha}}{\sum_{c\in\mathcal{A}}\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(c)\,u_{x}(c)^{\,2-\alpha}}=\frac{\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)\,\bigl[1+(\alpha-1)\bigl(x(a)-\mu_{x}\bigr)\bigr]^{(2-\alpha)/(\alpha-1)}}{\sum_{c\in\mathcal{A}}\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(c)\,\bigl[1+(\alpha-1)\bigl(x(c)-\mu_{x}\bigr)\bigr]^{(2-\alpha)/(\alpha-1)}}\,.

Jensen-Shannon.

Let
f(u)=\frac{1}{2}\left(u\log(u)-(u+1)\log(\frac{u+1}{2})\right)
. In this case, we have

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(u)=\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{2u}{u+1}\right)\kern 5.0pt,\quad f^{\prime\prime}(u)=\frac{1}{2u(u+1)}\kern 5.0pt,\quad[f^{\prime}]^{-1}(u)=\frac{\exp(2u)}{2-\exp(2u)}\kern 5.0pt.

The
\operatorname{JS-softargmax}
operator specialize (with base measure
\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})
) to

\nu_{x}^{JS}(a)=\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)[f^{\prime}]^{-1}(x(a)-\mu_{x})=\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)\frac{\exp(2(x(a)-\mu_{x}))}{2-\exp(2(x(a)-\mu_{x}))},\qquad a\in\mathcal{A},

where
\mu_{x}
is the unique normalizer satisfying the constraint

\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\nu_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)\frac{\exp(2(x(a)-\mu_{x}))}{2-\exp(2(x(a)-\mu_{x}))}=1,

B.3
Derivatives of the policy

Next, we exploit the expression of the derivatives of the soft-
f
-argmax derived in
Lemma

B.4
to compute the first and second derivatives of the policy. We begin by extending the notations defined in (
30
), (
31
), and (
32
) to encompass a dependence on the state.
For any pair
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
, define the first three derivatives of
f
evaluated at the likelihood ratio
\pi_{\theta}^{f}(a|s)/\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(a|s)
:

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{\theta}(a|s)

\displaystyle\;:=\;f^{\prime}_{\theta(s,\cdot)}(a)=f^{\prime}\!\left(\frac{\pi_{\theta}^{f}(a|s)}{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(a|s)}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

(38)

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime}_{\theta}(a|s)

\displaystyle\;:=\;f^{\prime\prime}_{\theta(s,\cdot)}(a)=f^{\prime\prime}\!\left(\frac{\pi_{\theta}^{f}(a|s)}{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(a|s)}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

(39)

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\theta}(a|s)

\displaystyle\;:=\;f^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\theta(s,\cdot)}(a)=f^{\prime\prime\prime}\!\left(\frac{\pi_{\theta}^{f}(a|s)}{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(a|s)}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

(40)

In addition, for every
s\in\mathcal{S}
we introduce the quantities

\displaystyle\operatorname{W}_{\theta}^{f}(s)\;:=\;\operatorname{W}_{\theta(s,\cdot)}^{f}=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\frac{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(a|s)}{f^{\prime\prime}_{\theta}(a|s)},\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{Y}_{\theta}^{f}(s)\;:=\;\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\frac{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(a|s)}{f^{\prime\prime}_{\theta}(a|s)}\,\left|f^{\prime}_{\theta}(a|s)\right|\kern 5.0pt.

(41)

For every
