). Next, observe that

\displaystyle\frac{4}{(1-\gamma)^{2}}+\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\log\left(\frac{297\omega_{f}^{2}C_{1}^{f}}{\epsilon d(\epsilon)(1-\gamma)^{6}\rho_{\min}^{2}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\!\!\leq\frac{4}{(1-\gamma)^{2}}+\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\log\left(\frac{19008\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{4\alpha-2}|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|}{\epsilon\alpha^{2}(1-\gamma)^{6}\rho_{\min}^{2}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{2}}\right)+\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\log\left(\exp_{\alpha}\left(-\frac{384|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|}{\epsilon\alpha^{2}(1-\gamma)^{3}\rho_{\min}}\right)^{4-2\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle\!\!\leq\frac{4}{(1-\gamma)^{2}}+\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\log\left(\frac{19008\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{4\alpha-2}|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|}{\epsilon\alpha^{2}(1-\gamma)^{6}\rho_{\min}^{2}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{2}}\right)+\frac{1536|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|}{\epsilon\alpha^{2}(1-\gamma)^{4}\rho_{\min}}\kern 5.0pt,

where in the last inequality, we used that
\log(1+u)\leq x
for
u>-1
. This shows that our condition on
H
implies the one assumed in
Theorem

E.9
. Using (
109
), we have

\displaystyle\min\left(\frac{(1-\gamma)^{3}}{C_{f}^{(2)}},\frac{\epsilon^{2}d(\epsilon)(1-\gamma)^{6}B\rho_{\min}^{2}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{2}}{C_{f}^{(3)}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\geq\min\left(\frac{(1-\gamma)^{3}}{96\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{2\alpha-2}},\frac{\epsilon^{2}\alpha^{2}(1-\gamma)^{6}B\rho_{\min}^{2}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{2}}{13824\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{5\alpha-5}|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|}\exp_{\alpha}\left(-\frac{384|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|}{\epsilon\alpha^{2}(1-\gamma)^{3}\rho_{\min}}\right)^{4-2\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\geq\frac{\epsilon^{2}\alpha^{2}(1-\gamma)^{6}B\rho_{\min}^{2}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{2}}{13824\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{5\alpha-5}|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|}\exp_{\alpha}\left(-\frac{384|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|}{\epsilon\alpha^{2}(1-\gamma)^{3}\rho_{\min}}\right)^{4-2\alpha}\kern 5.0pt,

(110)

where in the last inequality, we used the condition on
B
introduced in (
106
). Hence our condition on the step-size ensures that the one assumed in
Theorem

E.9
is satisfied. Next, using (
109
) and (
110
) yields

\displaystyle\frac{16C_{f}^{(1)}\ell(\epsilon)}{\epsilon d(\epsilon)(1-\gamma)^{2}\rho_{\min}^{2}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{2}}\max\left(\frac{C_{f}^{(2)}}{(1-\gamma)^{3}},\frac{C_{f}^{(3)}}{\epsilon^{2}d(\epsilon)(1-\gamma)^{6}B\rho_{\min}^{2}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{16C_{f}^{(1)}\ell(\epsilon)}{\epsilon d(\epsilon)(1-\gamma)^{2}\rho_{\min}^{2}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{2}}\cdot\frac{13824\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{5\alpha-5}|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|}{\epsilon^{2}\alpha^{2}(1-\gamma)^{6}B\rho_{\min}^{2}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{2}}\exp_{\alpha}\left(-\frac{384|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|}{\epsilon\alpha^{2}(1-\gamma)^{3}\rho_{\min}}\right)^{2\alpha-4}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{1024\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{2\alpha-2}|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|\ell(\epsilon)}{\epsilon\alpha^{2}(1-\gamma)^{2}\rho_{\min}^{2}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{2}}\cdot\frac{13824\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{5\alpha-5}|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|}{\epsilon^{2}\alpha^{2}(1-\gamma)^{6}B\rho_{\min}^{2}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{2}}\exp_{\alpha}\left(-\frac{384|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|}{\epsilon\alpha^{2}(1-\gamma)^{3}\rho_{\min}}\right)^{4\alpha-8}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{12^{7}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{7\alpha-7}|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|^{2}}{\epsilon^{3}\alpha^{4}(1-\gamma)^{8}\rho_{\min}^{4}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{4}B}\exp_{\alpha}\left(-\frac{384|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|}{\epsilon\alpha^{2}(1-\gamma)^{3}\rho_{\min}}\right)^{4\alpha-8}\log\left(\frac{6(v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}^{f}(\rho)-v_{\hskip 0.57367pt{\theta_{0}}}^{f}(\rho))}{\epsilon}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

which proves that under our condition on
T
the one assumed by
Theorem

E.9
is satisfied.
∎

Corollary F.7
.

Assume the same condition of
Corollary

F.6
. For any
(1-\gamma)^{-1}>\epsilon>0
and
\alpha\in(0,1)
, denote respectively by
T(\epsilon,\alpha)
,
B(\epsilon,\alpha)
, and
H(\epsilon,\alpha)
, the thresholds set in
Corollary

F.6
on
T
,
B
, and
H
, to learn an
\epsilon
-solution of the unregularized problem. Addtionnaly, denote by
\alpha^{\star}(\epsilon)
the minimizer of
T(\epsilon,\alpha)B(\epsilon,\alpha)H(\epsilon,\alpha)
. It holds that

\displaystyle\alpha^{\star}(\epsilon)=\frac{11}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{\log(1/\epsilon)}+o\left(\frac{1}{\log(1/\epsilon)}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Additionally, for
\epsilon<e^{-11}
, it holds that

\displaystyle T(\epsilon,\alpha^{\star}(\epsilon))B(\epsilon,\alpha^{\star}(\epsilon))H(\epsilon,\alpha^{\star}(\epsilon))=\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|^{11}}{\epsilon^{12}(1-\gamma)^{36}\rho_{\min}^{14}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{11}}\log\left(\frac{(v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}^{f}(\rho)-v_{\hskip 0.57367pt{\theta_{0}}}^{f}(\rho))}{\epsilon}\right)\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

We first provide an equivalent of the
\alpha
that optimises the sample complexity provided in
Corollary

F.6
and then bound the sample complexity obtained by using this
\alpha
.

Finding the best
\alpha
.

Firstly, note that using
Corollary

F.6
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{S}(\epsilon,\alpha)

\displaystyle:=\log\left(T(\epsilon,\alpha)B(\epsilon,\alpha)H(\epsilon,\alpha)\right)

\displaystyle=\log\left(\frac{12^{7}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{7\alpha-7}|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|^{2}}{\epsilon^{3}\alpha^{4}(1-\gamma)^{8}\rho_{\min}^{4}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{4}}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{4\alpha-8}{\alpha-1}\log\left(1+(1-\alpha)\left(\frac{384|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|}{\epsilon\alpha^{2}(1-\gamma)^{3}\rho_{\min}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\log\left(\log\left(\frac{6(v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}^{f}(\rho)-v_{\hskip 0.57367pt{\theta_{0}}}^{f}(\rho))}{\epsilon}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\log\left(\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\log\left(\frac{19008\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{4\alpha-2}|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|}{\epsilon\alpha^{2}(1-\gamma)^{6}\rho_{\min}^{2}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{2}}\right)+\frac{1540|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|}{\epsilon\alpha^{2}(1-\gamma)^{4}\rho_{\min}}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Firstly, observe that for any function
k(\epsilon)
which does converge to a different value from
0
, we have

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{S(\varepsilon,1/\log(1/\varepsilon))}{S(\varepsilon,k(\varepsilon))}<1\kern 5.0pt,

which establishes that
\alpha^{\star}(\epsilon)\to 0
. This allows, to rewrite
\mathrm{S}(\epsilon,\alpha)
as

\displaystyle\mathrm{S}(\epsilon,\alpha)=\log\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{4}\alpha^{6}}\right)+\frac{8-4\alpha}{1-\alpha}\log\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon\alpha^{2}}\right)+\psi(\alpha,\epsilon)\kern 5.0pt,

where
\psi(\alpha,\epsilon)
is defined as

\displaystyle\psi(\alpha,\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\log\left(\frac{12^{7}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{7\alpha-7}|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|^{2}}{(1-\gamma)^{8}\rho_{\min}^{4}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{4}}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{4\alpha-8}{\alpha-1}\left[\log\left(1+(1-\alpha)\left(\frac{384|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|}{\epsilon\alpha^{2}(1-\gamma)^{3}\rho_{\min}}\right)\right)-\log\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon\alpha^{2}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\log\left(\log\left(\frac{6(v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}^{f}(\rho)-v_{\hskip 0.57367pt{\theta_{0}}}^{f}(\rho))}{\epsilon}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\log\left(\frac{\epsilon\alpha^{2}}{1-\gamma}\log\left(\frac{19008\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{4\alpha-2}|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|}{\epsilon\alpha^{2}(1-\gamma)^{6}\rho_{\min}^{2}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{2}}\right)+\frac{1540|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|}{(1-\gamma)^{4}\rho_{\min}}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Importantly, observe that
\psi(\alpha,\epsilon)
is dominated by
\log(1/\epsilon)
when
(\alpha,\epsilon)\to 0
and that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\psi(\alpha,\epsilon)}{\partial\alpha}=o\left(\frac{1}{\alpha\log(\frac{1}{\epsilon\alpha})}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Computing the derivative of this function with respect to
\alpha
yields

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathrm{S}(\epsilon,\alpha)}{\partial\alpha}

\displaystyle=\frac{-6}{\alpha}+4\frac{1}{(1-\alpha)^{2}}\log\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)+4\frac{1}{(1-\alpha)^{2}}\log\left(\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\right)-\frac{8}{\alpha}\left(1+\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\right)+\frac{\partial\psi(\epsilon,\alpha)}{\partial\alpha}

(111)

\displaystyle=\frac{-22}{\alpha}+4\log\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)+o\left(\frac{1}{\alpha\log(\frac{1}{\epsilon\alpha})}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

(112)

As

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathrm{S}(\epsilon,\alpha)}{\partial\alpha}\bigg|_{\alpha=\alpha^{\star}(\epsilon)}=0\kern 5.0pt,

Then this implies that

\displaystyle\alpha^{\star}(\epsilon)=\frac{11}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{\log(1/\epsilon)}+o\left(\frac{1}{\log(1/\epsilon)}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Next, we provide a bound on the sample complexity given by this
\alpha^{\star}(\epsilon)
.

Computing the sample complexity.

Firstly, note that for
\alpha\leq 1/2
and
\epsilon<1
, we have

\displaystyle T(\epsilon,\alpha)B(\epsilon,\alpha)H(\epsilon,\alpha)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{12^{11}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{7\alpha-7}|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|^{3}}{\epsilon^{4}\alpha^{6}(1-\gamma)^{1}2\rho_{\min}^{5}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{4}}\exp_{\alpha}\left(-\frac{384|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|}{\epsilon\alpha^{2}(1-\gamma)^{3}\rho_{\min}}\right)^{4\alpha-8}\log\left(\frac{6(v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}^{f}(\rho)-v_{\hskip 0.57367pt{\theta_{0}}}^{f}(\rho))}{\epsilon}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{12^{11}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{7\alpha-7}|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|^{3}}{\epsilon^{4}\alpha^{6}(1-\gamma)^{12}\rho_{\min}^{5}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{4}}\left(1+(1-\alpha)\frac{384|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|}{\epsilon\alpha^{2}(1-\gamma)^{3}\rho_{\min}}\right)^{(8-4\alpha)/(1-\alpha)}\log\left(\frac{6(v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}^{f}(\rho)-v_{\hskip 0.57367pt{\theta_{0}}}^{f}(\rho))}{\epsilon}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{7\alpha-7}|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|^{3}}{\epsilon^{4}\alpha^{6}(1-\gamma)^{12}\rho_{\min}^{5}\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}^{4}}\left(\frac{|\log(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})|}{\epsilon\alpha^{2}(1-\gamma)^{3}\rho_{\min}}\right)^{(8-4\alpha)/(1-\alpha)}\log\left(\frac{6(v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}^{f}(\rho)-v_{\hskip 0.57367pt{\theta_{0}}}^{f}(\rho))}{\epsilon}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

(113)

Next, note that for
\epsilon<e^{-11}
, we have
\alpha=\frac{11}{2\log(1/\epsilon)}\leq 1/2
. Thus, using that
1/(1-\alpha)\leq 1+2\alpha
yield

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon\alpha^{2}}\right)^{(8-4\alpha)/(1-\alpha)}=\exp\left(\frac{8-4\alpha}{1-\alpha}\cdot\log\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon\alpha^{2}}\right)\right)\leq\exp\left((8+8\alpha)\cdot\log\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon\alpha^{2}}\right)\right)\lesssim\frac{1}{\epsilon^{8}\alpha^{16}}\kern 5.0pt.

Plugging in the previous bound in (
113
) concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix G
Discussion on unregularized Policy Gradient with
f
-SoftArgmax Parameterization

In this section, we show that it is possible to derive Non-Uniform Łojasiewicz inequalities on the unregularized objective
v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}(\rho):=v_{\hskip 0.57367pt{\pi_{\theta}^{f}}}(\rho)
under
f
-SoftArgmax Parameterization in the bandit setting. We expect the analysis to extend to the RL setting using similar arguments to those of
[
21
,
20
,
17
]
.

Theorem G.1
.

Consider the bandit case, i.e.
|\mathcal{S}|=1
. Assume that, for some
\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}>0
,
f
and
\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}
satisfy
\textbf{A}_{f}(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})
and
\textbf{P}(\underline{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}})
respectively. We also assume that the function
1/f^{\prime\prime}
is convex and that the initial distribution
\rho
satisfies
\textbf{A}_{\rho}
. Then, it holds that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}(\rho)\right\|_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{\operatorname{w}_{\theta}^{f}(a^{\star})^{2}}{\zeta_{f}}\frac{1}{v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}(\rho)-v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}(\rho)}\cdot f^{\prime\prime}\left(\frac{v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}(\rho)-v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}(\rho)}{v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}(\rho)-v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}(\rho)}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

where
v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}(\rho)=\max\limits_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})^{\mathcal{S}}}v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi}(\rho)
.

Proof.

Subsequently, we drop the dependency on the state for more clarity. Using
Lemma

C.2
(with
\gamma=0
and
|\mathcal{S}|=1
), for any
a\in\mathcal{A}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}(\rho)}{\partial\theta(a)}=\operatorname{w}_{\theta}^{f}(a)\cdot(\mathsf{r}(a)-\sum_{b}\operatorname{w}_{\theta}^{f}(b)\mathsf{r}(b)).

Denote by
a^{\star}
any optimal action, and
\mathsf{r}^{\star}=\mathsf{r}(a^{\star})
. We have

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}(\rho)\right\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{a}\operatorname{w}_{\theta}^{f}(a)^{2}\cdot\left(\mathsf{r}(a)-\sum_{b}\operatorname{w}_{\theta}^{f}(b)\mathsf{r}(b)\right)^{2}\geq\operatorname{w}_{\theta}^{f}(a^{\star})^{2}\left(\sum_{b}\operatorname{w}_{\theta}^{f}(b)(\mathsf{r}^{\star}-\mathsf{r}(b))\right)^{2}\,,

Using that
\Delta(a)=\mathsf{r}^{\star}-\mathsf{r}(a)\geq 0
, and also

\operatorname{w}_{\theta}^{f}(a)=\frac{1}{\operatorname{W}_{\theta}^{f}}\cdot\frac{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)}{f^{\prime\prime}(\pi_{\theta}^{f}(a)/\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(a))}\kern 5.0pt,\quad\operatorname{W}_{\theta}^{f}:=\sum_{b\in\mathcal{A}}\frac{\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)}{f^{\prime\prime}(\pi_{\theta}^{f}(b)/\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(b))}\kern 5.0pt,

yields

\displaystyle\sum_{b}\operatorname{w}_{\theta}^{f}(b)\Delta(b)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\operatorname{W}_{\theta}^{f}}\cdot\sum_{b\in\mathcal{A}}\Delta(b)\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(b)\cdot\frac{1}{f^{\prime\prime}(\pi_{\theta}^{f}(b)/\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(b))}.

Let’s introduce a distribution
\gamma(a)=\Delta(a)\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(a)/(\sum_{b\in\mathcal{A}}\Delta(b)\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(b))
, then we have

\displaystyle\sum_{b\in\mathcal{A}}\operatorname{w}_{\theta}^{f}(b)\Delta(b)=\frac{1}{\operatorname{W}_{\theta}^{f}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{c\sim\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}}[\Delta(c)]}\mathbb{E}_{c\sim\gamma}\left[1/(f^{\prime\prime}(\pi_{\theta}^{f}(c)/\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(c)))\right]\,.

Next, we use that a map
x\mapsto 1/f^{\prime\prime}(x)
is convex, and thus was have

\mathbb{E}_{c\sim\gamma}\left[1/(f^{\prime\prime}(\pi_{\theta}^{f}(c)/\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(c)))\right]\geq 1/f^{\prime\prime}\left(\mathbb{E}_{c\sim\gamma}\left[\pi_{\theta}^{f}(c)/\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(c))\right]\right)=1/f^{\prime\prime}\left(\frac{\sum_{c\in\mathcal{A}}\Delta(c)\pi_{\theta}^{f}(c)}{\sum_{c\in\mathcal{A}}\Delta(c)\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(c)}\right).

Overall, we have

\sum_{b\in\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\theta}^{f}(b)\Delta(b)\geq\frac{1}{\operatorname{W}_{\theta}^{f}}\frac{1}{\left(\sum_{c\in\mathcal{A}}\Delta(c)\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(c)\right)f^{\prime\prime}\left(\frac{\sum_{c\in\mathcal{A}}\Delta(c)\pi_{\theta}^{f}(c)}{\sum_{c\in\mathcal{A}}\Delta(c)\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(c)}\right)}\kern 5.0pt.

Finally, using that
\operatorname{W}_{\theta}^{f}\leq\zeta_{f}
concludes the proof.
∎

Similarly, to
[
21
,
18
,
17
]
, this Łojasiewicz inequality depends on the probability of the optimal action, which is very restrictive. Although extending the analysis of
[
21
,
18
,
17
]
in the deterministic setting is possible, addressing the stochastic setting for this type of Łojasiewicz inequality appears very challenging. This justifies adding a regularizer to the objective to ensure better PL inequalities, and on which the minimal coefficient can be lower bounded on the trajectory by leveraging a proper projection operator.

Appendix H
Links with Mirror Descent

To avoid overwhelming readers with technical details, we keep the discussion in this paragraph at a high level.
There is a clear connection between the coupled parameterization we consider and mirror descent (MD) algorithms. The discussion below is informal, meant to highlight the key ideas.
We stress that the proposed method is fundamentally different from mirror descent.
Let us define a mapping
\Phi(\pi)=\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\operatorname{D}^{f}(\pi(\cdot|s)\|\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(\cdot|s))
.
For the functions
f
that we consider,
\Phi
is Legendre on the positive orthant and separable across states
[
5
]
. In this case, the
f
-regularized value function
v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi}^{f}(\rho)
can be optimized directly in the policy space via the Lazy Mirror Descent algorithm (or dual averaging; see
24
,
34
,
12
) with
\Phi
as mirror map. Denoting by
\pi_{t}
the policy at step
t
and
\widetilde{\pi}_{t}
by the unnormalized policy at step
t
, the lazy MD updates reads:

\nabla\Phi(\widetilde{\pi}_{t+1})=\nabla\Phi(\widetilde{\pi}_{t})+\eta\nabla_{\pi}v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi}^{f}(\rho)|_{\pi=\pi_{t}}\,,\quad\pi_{t+1}=\argmin_{\pi\in\Pi}B_{\Phi}(\pi\|\widetilde{\pi}_{t+1})\,.

(114)

where
\Pi=\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})^{|\mathcal{S}|}
is a policy space and
B_{\Phi}(\pi\|\pi^{\prime})=\Phi(\pi)-\Phi(\pi^{\prime})-\langle\nabla\Phi(\pi^{\prime}),\pi-\pi^{\prime}\rangle
is the corresponding Bregman divergence. Since
\Phi
is separable over states, the Bregman projection can be written state-wise as
\pi_{t+1}(\cdot|s)=\operatorname{f-softargmax}(\nabla\Phi(\widetilde{\pi}_{t+1})(s,\cdot),\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(\cdot|s))
.

By denoting
\theta_{t}=\nabla\Phi(\widetilde{\pi}_{t})
, one obtains updates that resemble those of (
12
) (after the removal of
\mathcal{T}
), with one important difference: the gradient in (
114
) is taken with respect to the policy
\pi
whereas in (
12
) it is computed w.r.t the ”dual” parameter
\theta
(in the MD terminology). Even more important, the update (
114
) can be expressed as, by the chain rule

\textstyle\theta_{t+1}=\theta_{t}+\eta\left[\frac{\partial\pi_{\theta}^{f}}{\partial\theta}\big|_{\theta=\theta_{t}}\right]^{-1}\nabla_{\theta}J^{f}(\theta_{t})\,,

which have an additional preconditioning term given by the inverse of the policy Jacobian.

A crucial feature of (
12
) is that it performs a gradient ascent in the ”dual” space directly. This algorithm can be extended in the non-tabular setting directly, by parameterizing the function
\theta(s,a)
, allowing extensions to deep RL.
This is in contrast with Lazy-MD methods
[
24
,
34
,
12
]
, due to preconditioning, which cannot be expressed as direct parameter-space gradient steps. This remark has several important implications, which we leave for future work.

Appendix I
Technical Lemmas

Lemma I.1

(Lemma 1.2.3 in
23
)
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be twice continuously differentiable.
Suppose there exists
L\geq 0
such that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

|v^{\top}\nabla^{2}f(x)\,v|\;\leq\;L\|v\|^{2}.

Then
f
has an
L
-Lipschitz continuous gradient (i.e.,
f
is
L
-smooth); in particular,

\|\nabla f(y)-\nabla f(x)\|\;\leq\;L\|y-x\|,

and

f(y)\;\geq\;f(x)+\langle\nabla f(x),y-x\rangle-\tfrac{L}{2}\|y-x\|^{2}

for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Lemma I.2
.

Consider any two policies
\pi_{i}
,
i=1,2
. It holds that

\displaystyle\left\|d_{\rho}^{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi_{1}}-d_{\rho}^{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi_{2}}\right\|_{1}\leq\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma}\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left\|\pi_{1}(\cdot|s)-\pi_{2}(\cdot|s)\right\|_{1}\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

Let us start from the definition of flow conservation constraints for the discounted state visitation
[
28
]
, for
i\in\{1,2\}
, we have

\displaystyle d_{\rho}^{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi_{i}}(s)=(1-\gamma)\rho(s)+\gamma\sum_{s^{\prime}}\mathsf{P}_{\pi_{i}}(s|s^{\prime})d_{\rho}^{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi_{i}}(s^{\prime})\kern 5.0pt.

Then, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}|d_{\rho}^{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi_{2}}(s)-d_{\rho}^{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi_{1}}(s)|

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\sum_{(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})}\sum_{s}\left|\mathsf{P}(s|s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\pi_{2}(a^{\prime}|s^{\prime})d_{\rho}^{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi_{2}}(s^{\prime})-\mathsf{P}(s|s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\pi_{1}(a^{\prime}|s^{\prime})d_{\rho}^{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi_{1}}(s^{\prime})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\sum_{s^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\sum_{s}\mathsf{P}(s|s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\left|\pi_{2}(a^{\prime}|s^{\prime})-\pi_{1}(a^{\prime}|s^{\prime})\right|d_{\rho}^{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi_{2}}(s^{\prime})

\displaystyle+\gamma\sum_{s^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\sum_{s}\mathsf{P}(s|s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\pi_{1}(a^{\prime}|s^{\prime})\left|d_{\rho}^{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi_{1}}(s^{\prime})-d_{\rho}^{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi_{2}}(s^{\prime})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left\|\pi_{1}(\cdot|s)-\pi_{2}(\cdot|s)\right\|_{1}+\gamma\sum_{s^{\prime}}|d_{\rho}^{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi_{1}}(s^{\prime})-d_{\rho}^{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi_{2}}(s^{\prime})|\kern 5.0pt,

which concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma I.3

(Performance Difference Lemma)
.

It holds that

\displaystyle v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}^{f}(\rho)-v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}^{f}(\rho)=\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}d_{\rho}^{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi_{\star}^{f}}(s)\bigg[\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\star}^{f}(a|s)q_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}^{f}(s,a)-\lambda\operatorname{D}^{f}(\pi_{\star}^{f}(\cdot|s)\|\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(\cdot|s))-v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi_{\theta}}^{f}(s)\bigg]\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

Fix
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{S}||\mathcal{A}|}
and any state
s\in\mathcal{S}
. It holds that

\displaystyle v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}^{f}(s)-v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi_{\theta}}^{f}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\star}^{f}(a|s)q_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}^{f}(s,a)-\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\theta}^{f}(a|s)q_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}^{f}(s,a)

\displaystyle-\lambda\operatorname{D}^{f}(\pi_{\star}^{f}(\cdot|s)\|\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(\cdot|s))+\lambda\operatorname{D}^{f}({\pi_{\theta}^{f}}(\cdot|s)\|\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(\cdot|s))

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\star}^{f}(a|s)\left(q_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}^{f}(s,a)-q_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}^{f}(s,a)\right)+\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\left(\pi_{\star}^{f}(a|s)-\pi_{\theta}^{f}(a|s)\right)q_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}^{f}(s,a)

\displaystyle-\lambda\operatorname{D}^{f}(\pi_{\star}^{f}(\cdot|s)\|\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(\cdot|s))+\lambda\operatorname{D}^{f}({\pi_{\theta}^{f}}(\cdot|s)\|\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(\cdot|s))

\displaystyle=\gamma\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\star}^{f}(a|s)\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\mathsf{P}(s^{\prime}|s,a)\left(v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}^{f}(s^{\prime})-v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}^{f}(s^{\prime})\right)+\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\left(\pi_{\star}^{f}(a|s)-\pi_{\theta}^{f}(a|s)\right)q_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}^{f}(s,a)

\displaystyle-\lambda\operatorname{D}^{f}(\pi_{\star}^{f}(\cdot|s)\|\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(\cdot|s))+\lambda\operatorname{D}^{f}({\pi_{\theta}^{f}}(\cdot|s)\|\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(\cdot|s))\kern 5.0pt,

where in the last equality, we used the definition of the regularized Q-function (
2
). Expanding the recursion yields

\displaystyle v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}^{f}(s)-v_{\hskip 0.57367pt\pi_{\theta}}^{f}(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}d_{s}^{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}(s^{\prime})\left[\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\left(\pi_{\star}^{f}(a|s^{\prime})-\pi_{\theta}^{f}(a|s^{\prime})\right)q_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}^{f}(s^{\prime},a)\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}d_{s}^{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}(s^{\prime})\left[\lambda\operatorname{D}^{f}({\pi_{\theta}^{f}}(\cdot|s^{\prime})\|\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(\cdot|s^{\prime}))-\lambda\operatorname{D}^{f}(\pi_{\star}^{f}(\cdot|s^{\prime})\|\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(\cdot|s^{\prime}))\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}d_{s}^{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}(s^{\prime})\left[\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\star}^{f}(a|s^{\prime})q_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}^{f}(s^{\prime},a)-\lambda\operatorname{D}^{f}(\pi_{\star}^{f}(\cdot|s^{\prime})\|\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(\cdot|s^{\prime}))\right]

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}d_{s}^{\hskip 0.57367pt\star}(s^{\prime})\left[\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\theta}^{f}(a|s^{\prime})q_{\hskip 0.57367pt\theta}^{f}(s^{\prime},a)-\lambda\operatorname{D}^{f}({\pi_{\theta}^{f}}(\cdot|s^{\prime})\|\pi_{\operatorname{ref}}(\cdot|s^{\prime}))\right]\kern 5.0pt,

which concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma I.4

(Lemma 23 of
[
21
]
)
.

Let
\pi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})
. Denote
H(\pi)=\mathrm{diag}(\pi)-\pi\pi^{\top}
. For any vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{A}|}

\displaystyle\left\|H(\pi)\left(x-\frac{\langle x,\mathsf{1}_{|\mathcal{A}|}\rangle}{|\mathcal{A}|}\mathsf{1}_{|\mathcal{A}|}\right)\right\|_{2}\geq\min_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\pi(a)\cdot\left\|x-\frac{\langle x,\mathsf{1}_{|\mathcal{A}|}\rangle}{|\mathcal{A}|}\mathsf{1}_{|\mathcal{A}|}\right\|_{2}\kern 5.0pt.

Lemma I.5

(
7
)
.

Let
Z\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
be compact and let
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times Z\to\mathbb{R}
be continuous. Define

f(x)\;=\;\max_{z\in Z}\,\phi(x,z),\qquad Z_{0}(x)\;=\;\arg\max_{z\in Z}\,\phi(x,z).

Assume that for each fixed
z\in Z
, the map
x\mapsto\phi(x,z)
is differentiable. If
Z_{0}(x)=\{\bar{z}\}
and
x\mapsto\phi(x,\bar{z})
is differentiable at
x
, then
f
is differentiable at
x
with

\frac{\partial f(x)}{\partial x}\;=\;\frac{\partial\phi(x,\bar{z})}{\partial x}.

Appendix J
Experiments

J.1
Uncoupling the parameterization and the regularization

Figure
3
compares the regularized value landscapes induced by different couplings between policy parameterizations and regularizers. Both Softmax / Entropy Regularization (Figure
3(a)
) and Softmax /
0.1
-Tsallis Regularization (Figure
3(b)
) produce highly ill–conditioned objectives, characterized by wide flat plateaus separated by extremely sharp ridges. These geometries create large regions with vanishing gradients together with nearly singular directions, which are known to slow down and destabilize policy–gradient methods. Switching to the Tsallis SoftArgmax parameterization already improves the situation: under Entropy Regularization (Figure
3(c)
), the flat directions are reduced and the basin around the optimum becomes more pronounced. However, the most favorable geometry is obtained when Tsallis SoftArgmax is coupled with Tsallis regularization (Figure
3(d)
). In this matched Tsallis–Tsallis regime, the landscape becomes smooth, strongly curved, and well–conditioned, with a single broad basin leading to the optimum and no spurious flat regions or steep barriers. This alignment between the geometry induced by the parameterization and that of the regularizer yields an almost quadratic objective in logits, explaining why the Tsallis–Tsallis coupling provides the best convergence behavior.

\lambda
and the step-size

.

NChain and DeepSea.

We consider two canonical tabular exploration benchmarks.

NChain
i̧s a long-horizon chain environment in which the agent must repeatedly move in one direction to reach a terminal state with a large
+1
reward, while a small immediate reward equal to
+0.01
is available for moving in the opposite direction. As the chain length increases, the probability of discovering the optimal policy decays exponentially unless sufficient structured exploration is induced.
The
DeepSea
environment, described in
Section

5
, is a two-dimensional sparse-reward navigation task in which the agent must follow a precise sequence of actions to reach a distant rewarding state. Both environments therefore test the ability of a policy-gradient method to propagate credit over long horizons and through sparse feedback.

Results.

Figure
4
reports learning curves on both environments.
On
NChain
(top row,
Figures

4(a)
,
4(b)
and
4(c)
), the standard softmax–entropy policy gradient baseline performs competitively for the smallest instance (Size 10), but its performance degrades markedly as the chain length increases. In particular, for Size 15 a substantial performance gap opens up, and for Size 20 softmax converges slowly and remains far from optimal. In contrast,
f
-PG
with
\alpha<1
achieves substantially higher returns and converges much faster for intermediate horizons, most notably for Size 1,5 where a clear performance gap with the softmax entropy-regularized policy gradient emerges. For the longest chain (Size 20), Tsallis regularization continues to outperform the other baselines but exhibits a similarly slow convergence trend to softmax–entropy, reflecting the difficulty of the problem at this scale. Moreover, alternative regularization schemes fail completely on the longest
NChain
of size 20. Additionally, Escort policy gradients and Hadamard parameterizations plateau at very low returns across all chain lengths, indicating the need for additional exploration.

A similar pattern is observed in
DeepSea
(bottom row,
Figures

4(d)
and
4(e)
). For Size 10, Tsallis-regularized policies perform on par with the softmax–entropy baseline and in some cases converge faster. For the more challenging Size 15 instance, Tsallis policies with
\alpha<1
remain competitive. In this environment,
f
-PG
dominates all other baselines, which fail to make meaningful progress toward high-return policies.

Although no single Tsallis parameter
\alpha<1
is uniformly optimal across all problem sizes, a clear and robust trend emerges across both
NChain
and
DeepSea
:
for every environment, there exists an
\alpha<1
that strictly outperforms all alternative schemes and matches or exceeds the performance of softmax–entropy
. These results corroborate the observations of
Section

5
and confirm that jointly tailoring the policy parameterization and the regularization to the structure of the problem leads to improved empirical performance.

J.3
Full description of the deepRL experiments

We provide here a full description of the
\alpha
-Tsallis PPO
algorithm and the experimental setup of
Section

5
.

Algorithm description.

\alpha
-
Tsallis PPO
follows the same overall algorithmic structure as standard
PPO
, with only minor modifications: the softmax policy parameterization and entropy regularization are replaced by their Tsallis
\alpha
-softargmax and Tsallis
\alpha
-regularization counterparts. Specifically, the policy network outputs unnormalised action logits, which are mapped to a probability distribution via the Tsallis
\alpha
-softargmax instead of a usual softmax (see Section 3). At each interaction step, we compute the Tsallis divergence between the current policy and the uniform distribution over actions using the same coefficient
\alpha
, and subtract
\lambda\cdot\operatorname{D}^{f_{\alpha}}(\pi(\cdot|s)\|\mathrm{uniform})
(see
Table

2
for the expression of
f_{\alpha}
)
from the received reward.
3
3

3

This regularized reward is used in the computation of advantages and value targets for the
DeepSea
environment. The reason is that adding entropy to the rewards turns out to be critical for the method’s final performance, since the original softmax-PPO fails even in
DeepSea
of size
20
. Notice that it contrasts with an original PPO that adds entropy regularization only to a loss function and not to a reward. For
Noisy Carpole
, we use a standard implementation of PPO as a baseline.
. All other components, including the clipped surrogate objective, value loss, and advantage normalization, remain unchanged.

Training pipeline.

At each update, we collect trajectories from
16
parallel environments for
32
steps, followed by
16
epochs of PPO optimisation over
4
minibatches. We use a discounting factor
\gamma=0.99
and GAE
\lambda=0.95
, and apply gradient clipping at norm
0.5
. Both actor and critic are two-layer multilayer perceptrons with
64
hidden units and
tanh
activations. Optimization is performed using
Adam

[
14
]
, and we perform a grid search over

\lambda\in\{10^{-3},\,10^{-2},\,10^{-1},\,1.0\},\quad\eta\in\{10^{-4},\,3\times 10^{-4},\,10^{-3}\}.

Each configuration is evaluated across
25
seeds. Episode returns are aggregated per configuration and reported as mean
\pm
standard error. For each
\alpha
, we select the configuration that produces the highest last reward on average.
